Welcome to Explore!
…the most comprehensive guide to discovering The Last Green Valley National 
Heritage Corridor and beyond. Together the 35 towns and cities of eastern 
Connecticut and south-central Massachusetts that are The Last Green Valley weave 
a tapestry of opportunities for adventurers, families, romantics and those who 
want to travel the scenic path. We have more than 500+ miles of trails to tackle on 
foot, mountain bike, snowshoes or even a horse! Our rivers and streams, including 
more than 90 miles of National Recreation Water Trails, offer seclusion and peace 
for an easy paddle all the way to the challenge of whitewater.  And the fishing is 
among the best you will find. Step into American history here. Dine on the freshest 
foods, sourced from a farm just minutes away, sip wine overlooking rolling hills and 
uncover artistry living in our valleys and on our hills.
The Last Green Valley is wide open for any adventure you choose. Explore! is your 
guide to enjoying and caring for this unique National Heritage Corridor, so together 
we can pass it on to future generations!

Explore! is published annually and while we 
have tried to make all information in this guide 
accurate and as up-to-date as possible, we assume 
no liability for errors or omissions. Please check 
with each establishment before visiting to avoid 
disappointment, since we cannot be responsible for 
changes or cancellations.

PAGE 6
Moving forward to 1994, The Last Green Valley 
National Heritage Corridor was designated 
and signed into existence by President Bill 
Clinton as only the 4th heritage area in the 
nation. Our Heritage Corridor redefined the 
entire program, having been conceived and 
willed into existence by a large group of 
passionate residents representing history, 
agriculture, the waterways, forests and 
economic development. Their passion and 
persistence changed how Congress defined 
a National Heritage Corridor, leaving its 
management in the hands of the community. 
It’s fitting that local determination was 
the most important factor in the region’s 
designation. This land has shaped a history 
and forged a community of trailblazers since 
the first Indigenous Peoples walked this 
region and that spirit is still at the heart of  
The Last Green Valley. 
 
The Last Green Valley, Inc. (TLGV) is the 
non-profit created by those residents to 
carry on the work of the Heritage Corridor 
and ensure what made this region worthy of 
being an area of national significance remains. 
Today there are 55 National Heritage Areas 
throughout the country. 
Learn more about 
National Heritage  
Areas at the National 
Park Service website  
nps.gov/heritageareas.
What is a  
National Heritage Corridor?
Explore! 
With the first National Heritage Area designated in 1984, a new idea for community-led 
conservation, heritage preservation and economic development was born. As President 
Ronald Reagan designated the first area in Illinois, he called the National Heritage Area 
program “a new kind of national park. ” 
P . Gessay

The Last Green Valley, Inc. (TLGV) staff and volunteers invite you to join the fun and learn about 
The Last Green Valley National Heritage Corridor by participating in our programs. Keep up to 
date on our website thelastgreenvalley.org and on our social media feeds.
TLGV Programs & Events
Explore! 
TLGV Acorn Adventures
Kids, bring your adults out to explore The Last 
Green Valley every month. Perfect for people 
12 and under — and sure to keep people 12 
and over entertained — Acorn Adventures 
will take us all across The Last Green Valley to 
dig in the mud and search streams for water 
critters, look toward the skies at our beautiful 
moon and twinkling stars, learn about birds of 
prey, be good stewards and much more! Visit 
thelastgreenvalley.org for more details. Acorn 
Adventures are sponsored by Centreville Bank.
TLGV Member Programs
Join Chief Ranger Bill Reid at monthly 
member programs varying from a mid-winter 
eagle survey to farm visits, historic site tours, 
night sky programs and more. Not yet a 
member? Join today! We have launched a new 
Pay-What-You-Can Membership program. 
As an added benefit, TLGV members receive 
In Touch, a magazine that highlights the 
special people, places, treasures and stories 
that make The Last Green Valley a National 
Heritage Corridor.
TLGV Rangers
Want to learn more about The Last Green 
Valley National Heritage Corridor? Contact  
our Chief Ranger Bill Reid and look for TLGV
Rangers at festivals, fairs and town celebra-
tions throughout the year.
Is your organization looking for an interesting 
and informative program? Consider a 
presentation about The Last Green Valley
National Heritage Corridor. TLGV currently 
offers 9 different presentations, each lasting 
approximately 45 minutes. Contact Chief
Ranger Bill Reid at Bill@tlgv.org or call him 
at 860-774-3300 for more information. The 
Ranger program is sponsored by Savers Bank.
Night Sky Rangers
Join TLGV’s team of Night Sky Rangers to help 
collect data on light pollution throughout the 
National Heritage Corridor. TLGV and our Lead 
Night Sky Rangers are available to assist mu-
nicipalities and businesses in reducing their 
light pollution. Contact TLGV Assistant  
Director Fran Kefalas at 860-774-3300 or 
Fran@tlgv.org to learn more.
    B. Reid

PAGE 8
TLGV Programs & Events
Spring Outdoors
Spring Outdoors brings thousands of people 
out of hibernation to explore all the National 
Heritage Corridor has to offer between the 
Vernal Equinox and the Summer Solstice. Join 
our partners and members on guided walks, 
bikes, paddles and tours during the entire 
season. Learn interesting stories and see 
things spring to life in The Last Green Valley.
Trail Assessment Team
TLGV’s Trail Assessment Team hits the trail 
every spring through fall to evaluate trails for 
accessibility. More than 20 miles of the Air 
Line Trail State Park have been assessed as 
well as boat launches and other locations. Visit 
TLGV’s website for accessibility information. 
Contact LyAnn Graff at LyAnn@tlgv.org or 
860-774-3300 to learn more about the team 
and its work.
Tastes of the Valley
Tastes of the Valley is an annual celebration 
for the taste buds of locally grown and 
produced foods and beverages created by 
some of the most innovative chefs, vintners, 
brewers and more from The Last Green 
Valley National Heritage Corridor. It is also a 
fundraiser for TLGV, supporting the work we 
do to help our local farms and food producers 
keep growing. 
Walktober
What started 32 years ago as a walking 
weekend with 22 adventures is now 300+ 
opportunities to explore The Last Green 
Valley National Heritage Corridor from the 
Autumnal Equinox through the first week 
of November. Walktober is the premiere fall 
event in Southern New England with walks, 
hikes, runs, pedals, paddles, climbs, stargazing 
and more for every interest. In between your 
adventures, use your Explore! guide or our 
online directory to eat, shop, visit, enjoy, learn 
and connect to The Last Green Valley!
Water Quality Monitoring
TLGV’s citizen science teams of water quality 
monitoring volunteers provide data that 
shape efforts to restore and preserve the 
region’s waterways. TLGV is always looking 
for more citizen scientists to help monitor 
the health of our streams, rivers, ponds and 
lakes. Whether you would like to monitor 
once a season, once a month or once a week, 
we have an opportunity for you. Training 
and equipment are provided. Together, we 
can identify, track and correct problems 
that might otherwise go undetected. 
For more information contact Jean Pillo, 
TLGV’s Volunteer Water Quality Monitoring 
Coordinator at Jean.Pillo@comcast.net
    J. Pillo

The 35 towns and cities of The Last Green Valley National Heritage Corridor each have 
adventures to offer and unique and interesting stories to tell. Together they are an 
extraordinary region tied together by the waters, forests, farms, history and people. 
35 Special Places
Explore! 
9 MASSACHUSETTS TOWNS
Brimfield (1731) (brimfieldma.org): Known  
for its huge annual antiques show, held 3 
times a year, you can enjoy award-winning, 
locally-made wines, ciders and beers after a 
day of outdoor adventures in Brimfield State 
Forest. Hike, fish, hunt and snowmobile or 
paddle across East Brimfield Lake, part of  
the Quinebaug River National Recreation 
Water Trail.
Charlton (1755) (townofcharlton.net): Visit the 
U.S. Army Corps of Engineers’ Buffumville Lake 
for year-round fun and programs or visit the 
86-acre Capen Hill Nature Sanctuary with trails 
and Visitor Center with live animal exhibits. 
Scenic views abound from Charlton’s hilltops 
and July means a blueberry festival in town.
Dudley (1731) (dudleyma.gov): History and 
hiking are easy to find here. The Black Tavern 
hosts programs year-round, and The Dudley 
Conservation Land Trust has conserved 
almost 350 acres and has miles of trails to 
explore. You’ll also find plenty to do at the 
Pearle L. Crawford Library, a state-of-the-art 
facility where you will be greeted by friendly 
faces and tons of programs for all ages. Don’t 
miss the annual strawberry festival.
East Brookfield (1920) (eastbrookfieldma.
us): The northernmost town in The Last 
Green Valley and the youngest town in the 
Commonwealth, East Brookfield is known as 
the “baby town of the Commonwealth. ” Home 
to baseball legend Connie Mack, the center of 
town hugs beautiful Lake Lashaway. You’ll find 
plenty of fresh air in East Brookfield.
Holland (1783) (town.holland.ma.us): If you 
love the water, Holland is for you. Lake Siog 
has it all in every season — swimming, scuba
diving, paddling and fishing (on the ice 
too) beckon you. There is also a competitive 
water ski team, a vibrant community center 
and trails for miles of hikes, runs, bikes and 
snowshoeing. 
Oxford (1713) (oxfordma.us): History and 
the outdoors are abundant in Oxford. 
A Firefighter’s Museum, an abandoned 
Huguenot Fort and the Clara Barton 
Birthplace Museum entice explorers with a 
historical interest. Enjoy the outdoors as the 
Mid-State Trail passes through town and the 
U.S. Army Corps of Engineers’ Hodges Village 
Dam offers paddling, hiking and much more.
A. Dabrowski
9

PAGE 10
35 Towns
Southbridge (1816) (ci.southbridge.ma.us): 
One of the “cities” of The Last Green Valley, 
Southbridge’s industrial revolution past is 
alive in its architecture and museums. Learn 
how Southbridge became “The Eye of the 
Commonwealth” at the Optical Heritage 
Museum, dine in the downtown restaurants, 
hit the trails or paddle the Quinebaug River all 
within easy reach of the heart of downtown. 
You’ll find a stop on The Last Green Valley 
letterboxing trail here too. 
Sturbridge (1738) (sturbridge.gov): History 
and nature come together in Sturbridge. Old 
Sturbridge Village brings history to life and an 
active trail system keeps you moving so you 
can enjoy all the wonderful dining options 
guilt-free. You’ll also find luxury lodging, 
camping and more for a trip to remember.
Webster (1832) (webster-ma.gov): Home 
to Lake Chargoggagoggmanchauggagog-
gchaubunagungamaugg, the largest natural 
lake in southern New England (1,442 acres), 
Webster welcomes boaters, fishermen and 
bald eagles. The French River flows through 
town and there is an active hunting and 
fishing community. It’s also home of the new 
Samuel Slater Experience, a local book store, 
a stop on The Last Green Valley letterboxing 
trail and so much more! 
26 CONNECTICUT TOWNS
Ashford (1714) (ashfordtownhall.org): You can 
enter the land of the pixies in Ashford if you 
trek on the blue-blazed Nipmuck Trail to Pixies 
Falls in Nipmuck State Forest. Or tackle miles 
of trails on Joshua’s Trust properties before 
stopping at an orchard for some locally crafted 
spirits or attending a program at Horizon 
Wings, a non-profit wildlife rehabilitation 
center specializing in birds of prey.
Brooklyn (1786) (brooklynct.org): Brooklyn’s 
center is iconic New England with the entire 
district on the Register of National Historic 
Places. Visit the Brooklyn Historical Society 
Museum & Daniel Putnam Tyler Law Office 
while visiting the town center. New paddlers 
will also love Riverside Park canoe/kayak launch, 
which is easy to find and has great parking.
Canterbury (1703) (canterburyct.org): Enjoy a 
scenic drive on National Scenic Byway Rt. 169 
through the heart of town. You’ll see many 
buildings on the National Register of Historic 
Places, including the home of the Connecticut 
State Heroine, Prudence Crandall. You can 
also paddle the Quinebaug River with two 
launches in town. 
Chaplin (1822) (chaplinct.org): A place 
steeped in legend and beautiful in every 
season, Diana’s Pool is one of the many 
extraordinary stops along the Natchaug River 
that calls to campers & fishermen year after 
year. You can also find history throughout 
town and the amazing blue-blazed Natchaug 
Trail in Natchaug State Forest.
Coventry (1711) (coventryct.org): The western    
most town in the Heritage Corridor, State 
Hero Nathan Hale called Coventry home. 
The Hale Homestead is a CT Landmarks site 
and home to one of southern New England’s 
largest farmers markets, The Coventry 
Farmer’s Market. Hale State Forest is also a 
popular place to walk and mountain bike. 
Eastford (1847) (eastfordct.org): Jump on 
the blue-blazed Nipmuck Trail on Westford 
Rd. and you’ll be walking on part of the 
Old Connecticut Path, a trail blazed by 
the Indigenous People of the region long 
before the colonists came to town. If ancient 
cemeteries pique your interest start with 
Phoenixville Cemetery where Civil War hero 
Nathaniel Lyon is laid to rest. You can then 
hike the blue-blazed Natchaug Trail to the 
ruins of his old homestead.
Franklin (1716) (franklinct.com): The seven 
rolling hills of Franklin shape the town’s 
landscape and history. The people of Franklin 
have a rich history and deep roots, the 
descendants of the town’s first settler still living 
on the land 10 generations later. You’ll find 
beautiful vistas and trails through still wild 
land. Visit the Ashbel Woodward Museum to 
learn more about this quiet and beautiful town. 
10
Green leaf indicates member of TLGV

PAGE 11
continued on next page
35 Towns
Griswold (1815) (griswold-ct.org): Spend the 
day or camp and spend a week at Hopeville 
Pond State Park, where you can picnic, paddle, 
hike, bike, swim and fish. You’ll find plenty 
of spots in town to fuel up with a hearty 
breakfast, lunch or dinner. The town’s active 
Recreation Department hosts events many 
times a year and if you prefer your adventure 
off a trail you can check out the skate park. 
Hampton (1786) (hamptonct.org): A historic 
town center has wonderful architecture but 
not a single stop light. The Air Line State 
Park Trail and Goodwin State Forest will give 
you plenty of reasons to stop and enjoy the 
scenery. Or take a walk in the footsteps of 
naturalist and author Edwin Way Teale at 
Connecticut Audubon’s Trailwood, Teale’s 
home that he donated to the non-profit. 
Killingly (1708) (killingly.org): Killingly 
has something for everyone — a historic 
downtown, shopping, dining, parks, rural 
areas, the Quinebaug River and year–
round events thanks to an active business 
association, town staff and volunteers. Home 
to one of the best locations to watch the 
sunrise, you’ll also find one of the locations on 
The Last Green Valley’s letterboxing trail  
in town too. 
Lebanon (1700) (lebanonct.gov): The iconic 
Lebanon Town Green is the home of history, a 
vibrant farmer’s market, wonderful antiques 
sale and much more. Want to stand where 
George Washington did? You can in Lebanon. 
The Historical Society Museum & Visitors 
Center offers engaging displays and hands-on 
activities for the whole family and there are 
several other historic sites lining the green to 
visit. You’ll also find one of the locations on 
The Last Green Valley’s letterboxing trail in 
town too.
Lisbon (1786) (lisbonct.com): National Scenic 
Byway Rt. 169 goes right down the middle of 
town. With the Shetucket River to the west 
and the Quinebaug River to the east, the 
views are indeed scenic. Lisbon’s trails bring 
together its bountiful natural world and its 
historic places, such as Burnham Tavern.
Mansfield/Storrs (1702) (mansfieldct.gov): 
The home of UConn and its rich cultural and 
historical venues, there’s plenty to do, buy, eat 
and drink in Storrs. Mansfield Hollow State 
Park and Mansfield Hollow Dam, one of six 
Army Corps properties in the Heritage Corridor, 
offer the opportunity to get away from all the 
action. The town’s commitment to open space 
also ensures a trail is never far away.
Norwich (1659) (norwichct.org): The oldest 
city in The Last Green Valley, the history of the 
nation, from the early relationships between 
Indigenous people and the colonists to the 
American and Industrial Revolutions, can be 
told in Norwich. The home of Benedict Arnold 
and Samuel Huntington, Norwich’s history is 
alive through its regular historic tours and its 
architecturally stunning downtown. You’ll find 
dining from around the world and a harbor 
where the bald eagles soar. 
Plainfield (1699) (plainfieldct.org): 
Rochambeau’s troops camped here for four 
days in 1781 on their way to meet George 
Washington and the town has been a place 
for travelers ever since. The East Coast 
Greenway and Quinebaug River National 
Recreation Water Trail pass through Plainfield, 
and there are several sites on the Washington-
Rochambeau National Trail here as well. 
Pomfret (1713) (pomfretct.gov): The legend 
of Israel Putnam and the last wolf in CT 
happened in Pomfret before the Revolutionary 
War. Today wildlife finds a haven in Pomfret 
thanks to the CT Audubon Society’s Bafflin 
Sanctuary, land trust properties, the Air Line 
State Park Trail and Mashamoquet Brook 
State Park. The town has also developed new 
mountain biking trails on one of its properties.
Preston (1686) (preston-ct.org): On the 
southern-most tip of The Last Green Valley, 
Preston is bounded by Poquetanuck Cove  
and the Quinebaug and Shetucket Rivers, 
providing important food sources and 
habitat for bald eagles. Of course this means 
great bird watching, fishing and paddling 
opportunities are available to you too.
11

PAGE 12
35 Towns
Putnam (1855) (putnamct.us): A destination 
downtown offers unique shops, dining, 
entertainment and perhaps one of the most 
photographed waterfalls in all of Southern 
New England. The town and businesses 
work together to provide year-round events, 
closing Main Street for pedestrian-friendly 
celebrations or filling the banks of the 
Quinebaug River in Rotary Park with music.
Scotland (1857) (scotlandct.org): Founded by 
Isaac Magoon and named for his country of 
birth, Scotland hosts the annual Scotland
Highland Festival, held at the Edward Waldo 
House each Columbus Day weekend in 
Walktober. It’s also the birthplace of Samuel 
Huntington and has a museum at his 
homestead. State lands also offer a variety of 
hiking opportunities. 
Sprague (1861) (ctsprague.org): The 
Shetucket River meanders through Sprague, 
creating incredible views, great paddling and 
fabulous fishing, especially for salmon. The 
town’s efforts to conserve land has resulted in 
miles of trails in the Sprague Land Preserve. 
You’ll also find Baltic, an entire village listed 
on the National Register of Historic Places.
Sterling (1794) (sterlingct.us): Long vistas 
offer lovely views along Rt. 14A and from  
atop scenic Rt. 49. The Quandock and Moosup 
Rivers provided power to the mills in the 
early 1800s and an old rail trail connecting to 
Rhode Island’s trail system now offers a nice 
woodland walk with views of the Moosup. 
You’ll also find one of the largest corn mazes 
and an iconic farm in town.
Thompson (1785) (thompsonct.org): West 
Thompson Dam’s miles of trails and beautiful 
lake enhance a robust town-maintained trail
system. Visit the Tri-State Marker and the sight 
of a historic train wreck. Along with its history, 
agriculture and New England’s largest alpaca 
farm, Thompson has a little bit of something 
for everyone.
Union (1734) (unionconnecticut.org): 
Outdoor recreation rules in the least populous 
town in Connecticut. Nipmuck State Forest,
Bigelow Hollow State Park and Yale-Myers 
Forest comprise 1/3 of the town’s forest 
land (that’s 34,000 acres). The town’s motto? 
Smallest town, biggest heart.
Voluntown (1721) (voluntown.gov): An 
outdoorsperson’s playground, Voluntown is 
more than 70 percent state forestland and 
the heart of Pachaug State Forest, the largest 
forest in CT. You can camp, glamp or backpack 
like a true thru hiker in Voluntown. A farmer’s 
market can fuel your outdoor adventures and 
a historic CCC camp awaits your discovery. 
Windham/Willimantic (1692) (windhamct.com): 
A true mill town, Windham and its city 
within, Willimantic, boast incredible Victorian 
architecture, the famous Frog Bridge, a 
vibrant downtown food scene and a new 
outdoor stage. Home to Eastern Connecticut 
State University, the Air Line Trail makes its 
way through town and you’ll find one of The 
Last Green Valley Letterbox Trail stops here. 
The town’s Lauter Park also boasts a skate and 
BMX park designed by skaters for skaters. 
Woodstock (1686) (woodstockct.gov): 
Area-wise, as the second largest town 
in CT, Woodstock is home to numerous 
working farms offering fresh food, CSAs and 
much more. Start your Bull Hill hike on the 
Woodstock side of this Wyndham Land Trust 
property, visit the amazing Pink House (aka 
Roseland Cottage), or stroll through Roseland 
Park and Palmer Arboretum.

Scenic Route 169  
–A National Scenic Byway
Explore! 
Stretching more than 47 miles from Norwich, 
CT to Charlton, MA, Route 169 is a drive 
through history, a slice of life in Southern 
New England and an opportunity to escape 
to a quieter, more peaceful place.  Just a note, 
Route 169 is known by many street names as 
it traverses the National Heritage Corridor.
The information here is listed from  
south to north along Route 169  
and is color-coded by interest: 
n History 
n Outdoor Activities  
n Tastes, Sips, Retail & Entertainment
Start your historic trek at the southern end 
of Rt. 169 at Leffingwell House Museum, 
348 Washington St., Norwich, CT, located 
across the road from the beginning of Rt. 169. 
Built in 1675, the museum’s architecture and 
history tell the story of the colonies and early 
America. Learn more on pgs. 54 and 112.
If you enjoy history, there are many 
Cemeteries, some pre-dating the nation, 
dotting Rt. 169 along its length that are 
fascinating places to explore.
Norwich’s Mohegan Park is an oasis in the 
city featuring swimming, walking trails, 
disc golf, several playgrounds and the city’s 
famous Rose Garden. See pgs. 47, 86 and  
163 for more information.
Ouellet Park is just off Rt. 169 at 20 Old 
Canterbury Tpke., Norwich. The park gives 
access to fishing at Taftville Reservoir and has 
a playscape and playing fields. See pg. 39 for 
more information.
Lisbon Meadows Park & Trails has parking 
right on Rt. 169 (just south of the Rt. 138/169 
intersection) in Lisbon and features trails, 
playing fields and open fields for family-fun. 
Learn more on pgs. 47 and 85.
In Lisbon you will find the John Bishop 
House at 11 Rt. 169, an 11-room Georgian 
post-and-beam farmhouse built in 1810. The 
John Bishop House is a slice of the agricultural 
history of Southern New England. See pg. 112 
to learn more.
A small detour on Rt. 138 will bring you to the 
Anshei Israel Synagogue, 142 Newent Rd., 
Lisbon, which is designated on the National 
Register of Historic Places. Pg. 112 offers more 
information.
13

PAGE 14
Scenic Route 169
The Burnham Tavern sits at 223 North 
Burnham Hwy, Lisbon, and was built in the 
early 1700s. It became an important stop 
for colonial troops during the American 
Revolution. While currently closed for 
renovations, the property leads to about 
1.5 miles of walking trails marked with 
interpretative signs. For more information,  
see pg. 112.
State Heroine Prudence Crandall ran a private 
school for girls in her home at the intersection 
Rts. 169 and 14 in Canterbury, which is now 
The Prudence Crandall Museum at 1 South 
Canterbury Rd., Canterbury. At the behest 
of Norwich’s Sarah and Mary Harris, Crandall 
began educating young Black women, which 
led to Crandall being arrested and threats on 
her life and those of her students. See pg. 114 
to learn more.
Paddle the Quinebaug River National 
Recreation Water Trail from the boat launch 
on Butts Bridge Rd. in Canterbury, less than a 
half-mile from Rt. 169. See pg. 128 for more 
information.
The Finnish American Heritage Society, 
76 North Canterbury Rd., Canterbury, tells 
the story of Finnish American immigration 
and its role in the region. The Society also 
offers many programs throughout the year, 
including the chance to experience Finnish 
food at its finest. See pg. 109 for more 
information.
The last weekend of August means The 
Brooklyn Fair with the fairgrounds right  
on the National Scenic Byway. 
The Brooklyn Historical Society sits in the 
heart of Brooklyn’s National Historic District 
at 25 Canterbury Rd., Brooklyn. The museum 
shares the exploits of Revolutionary War Gen. 
Israel Putnam, while the law office is the 19th 
century office of Putnam's great-grandson 
Daniel Putnam Tyler and is furnished as it 
might have been during his career. Learn 
more on pgs. 48 and 107.
Davis Forest & Disc Golf Course is just a few 
minutes off Rt. 169 at the end of Salmon Dr. 
in Brooklyn and features an 18-hole forested 
disc golf course and walking trails. Learn more 
on pg. 46.
Lapsley Orchard at 403 Orchard Hill Rd., 
Pomfret Center, features a classic New 
England farm stand open mid-July through 
Dec. 24th. See pgs. 64 and 120  for more 
information. 
Mashamoquet Brook State Park/Wolf Den, 
147 Wolf Den Dr. (Rt. 44), just off Rt. 169, is 
where the outdoors and history meet. Hike 
a 4.75-mile loop to the Wolf Den, a site on 
the National Register of Historic Places. Enjoy 
numerous other outdoor adventures. Find 
more information on pgs. 30 and 88. 
If your travels have you thirsty, Watercure 
Farm Distillery, 426 Mashamoquet Rd., 
Pomfret Center, has spirits for you that are 
distilled right in front of you. See pgs. 66, 137 
and 168 for more information.
Bring binoculars when you visit the 
Connecticut Audubon Center at Pomfret, 
218 Day St., Pomfret, for spectacular sightings 
of birds and other wildlife on this beautiful 
property. Audubon runs programs year-
round. Learn more on pgs. 87, 100 and 118.
You won’t miss the stunning connecting 
bridge that takes The Air Line State Park 
Trail over Rt. 169 in Pomfret. The Air Line Trail 
is a great place to walk, run, bike and more 
from a parking area off Railroad St. in Pomfret 
Center. See pgs. 28, 72, 94, 102, 153 and 174,
for more about the iconic Air Line Trail.
If trail races in iconic locations interest 
you, Tackle The Trail supports the QVCC 
Foundation and is held during Walktober on 
the Air Line Trail. Learn more on pgs. 57, 75 
and 121.
You’ll find breakfast, lunch and dinner and live 
music at the Vanilla Bean Café, 450 Deerfield 
Rd., Rt. 97, Pomfret, or a sweet treat to keep 
you fueled for more adventures. Learn more 
on pgs. 24 and 168.
Surprise your loved one with a bouquet of 
fresh flowers from Garden Gate Florist at 30 
Putnam Rd., Pomfret. Learn more on pgs. 135 
and 173.

A labyrinth awaits visitors to the First

For fresh organic produce and flowers stop 
at Echo Farm’ s farm stand, 1051 Rt. 169, 
Woodstock. Please see pg. 62 for more

Crossing into Massachusetts, look for signs for 
the Claire Birtz Wildlife Sanctuary on Tipton 
Rock Rd., Southbridge. Enjoy winding trails 
along the shores of Morse Pond. For more

The Quinebaug Valley Rail Trail — 
Southbridge Section can be accessed just off

trails project will extend 11 miles. For more 
information see pg. 77.
Not far from Rt. 169, The Optical Heritage 
Museum, 12 Crane St., Southbridge, 
highlights incredible moments in history and 
scientific discoveries that changed the world. 
Learn more on pg. 113.
Enjoy an outdoor adventure on wheels at the

Explore Capen Hill Nature Sanctuary, 56 
Capen Rd., Charlton, and its 86-acres of trails 
from dawn to dusk. Learn more on pg 49.

Explore! 
The Washington-Rochambeau  
Revolutionary Route
In 1778, the French formally allied with the 
colonists, but it was not until the summer 
of 1780 that troops under the command of 
General Jean Baptiste Donatien de Vimeur, 
comte de Rochambeau arrived in Newport, 
RI. General George Washington and General 
Rochambeau agreed joint military efforts 
should not begin until spring of 1781 so the 
French forces wintered in New England. 
The first stop on the Trail is two stops in one 
and actually the last place French troops 
stayed in Connecticut on their way home 
after the Revolution. In November 1782, in 
what was then Voluntown but is now Sterling, 
French troops camped on Sterling Hill. Known 
along the trail as French Infantry Campsite 
49 (north) in Voluntown it is the 49th 
campsite of the French forces as they returned 
from Virginia. The camp was in the fields 
east of Dorrance Tavern on the north side of 
Plainfield Pike (Rt. 14A) in Sterling. There is 
a marker for Camp 49 at 751 Plainfield Pike, 
Sterling, CT. 
 
Samuel Dorrance Inn/Dorrance Tavern, 
748 Plainfield Pike (Rt. 14A), Sterling, CT is 
where the officers stayed and dined. The 
officers included the marquis de Chastellux 
and comte de Rochambeau. Baron Ludwig 
von Closen, aide-de-camp to General 
Rochambeau, kept a journal and wrote of 
their march through Connecticut, “We have, 
on the whole, been treated wonderfully well 
wherever the army has marched. ”
On their way to Yorktown, VA, the French 
forces made their first stop in Connecticut 
at French Infantry Campsite 3 (south) in 
Plainfield where they stayed from June 
19-23, 1781. From written accounts, about 30 
local families were welcoming, and the locals 
and troops shared music, dance and food. 
There are two plaques commemorating the 
campsite: a bronze plaque erected in 1929 
on the east side of Rt. 12 about 0.4 miles 
north of the First Congregational Church, 
commemorating the Dorrance Homestead  
 
The Washington-Rochambeau Revolutionary Route is a National Historic Trail with 
locations from Massachusetts to Virginia. It commemorates the journey American and 
French forces made to and from the Seige of Y orktown, Virginia in 1781 and 1782.  
This list of sites includes all those located in The Last Green Valley. It begins  
on the border with Rhode Island then travels west to Lebanon, CT. 
To learn more about the Washington-Rochambeau National Historic Trail visit:
•  w3r-us.org
•   portal.ct.gov/DECD/Content/Historic-Preservation/01_Programs_Services/ 
Historic-Trails/Washington-Rochambeau-Revolutionary-Route
    N. Velles
16
Green leaf indicates member of TLGV

PAGE 17
and the Captain Eleazar Cady House, locations 
used by French officers; and another plaque 
on the north-east corner of Gallup St. and  
Rt. 12 commemorating the site of the  
Eaton Tavern.
Traveling to the next site in Canterbury, the 
French Encampment at Westminster is 
another location where the French stayed 
after Yorktown. Nov. 7-8, 1782 marked the 
48th encampment of the French forces on 
their way to Boston. There is a marker at 
Westminster Congregational Church, 395 
Westminster Rd. (Rt. 14), Canterbury, CT.
In 1781 the French troops marched from 
Plainfield to Windham for their next 
encampment, passing the birthplace 
of Samuel Huntington, a signer of the 
Declaration of Independence. See pg. 110 
to learn more about the Huntington 
Homestead.  
The French stopped in Windham both going 
to and coming from Virginia in support of the 
colonists in the Revolution. French Infantry 
Campsite 4 is the location the troops camped 
June 20-23, 1781 on their way to Virginia. They 
camped next to the Shetucket River. 
On Nov. 5-7, 1782 at French Infantry 
Campsite 47 (north) in Windham, French 
forces made their second encampment 
in town on their way back to Providence. 
This site is east of the first site. On Rt. 14 
next to a stonewall-lined field is a plaque 
commemorating both encampments. Neither 
camp was at the exact location, but this is the 
closest roadside marker.
As the troops left Windham they journeyed 
to Bolton, just outside the Heritage Corridor. 
However, another site in The Last Green Valley 
played a vital role in both the French effort in 
the Revolution and the colonial planning of 
the Revolution. 
From Windham make your way to the iconic 
Lebanon Town Green. From Nov. 1780 to 
June 1781, Lebanon provided winter quarters 
for 220 French cavalry soldiers known as 
hussars of Lauzun’s Legion. Connecticut 
Governor Jonathan Trumbull also held more 
than 1,000 meetings in what we now know 
as the War Office. Those who met at the 
War Office include George Washington, 
Benjamin Franklin, Thomas Jefferson, John 
Adams, Henry Knox, Marquis de Lafayette, 
Rochambeau, Marquis de Chastelux, Israel 
Putnam, Samuel Parsons, Joseph Spencer, 
Jedediah Huntington and Duc de Lauzun.
You can learn much more about Lebanon’s 
role in the Revolution from the Lebanon 
Historical Society, see pg. 23 for details. 
For more history along the trail, you can 
visit the Plainfield Historical Society in 
Plainfield, The Prudence Crandall Museum 
in Canterbury, and the Mill Museum in 
Willimantic. See Museums and Historic Sites 
pgs. 107-114.
If you would like to enjoy a bite to eat not 
far from the Trail, Ekonk Hill Turkey Farm 
in Sterling has a café and market, see pg 62 
for more information. Willimantic Brewing 
Company in Willimantic offers sit-down 
meals, craft brews and more, see pg. 170.
The Washington-Rochambeau Trail
    F. Kefalas

440 Main St., Willimantic, CT 06226 
gardenclubofwindhamct.org,

The Garden on the Bridge attracts visitors 
from the region and beyond. Built in 1857, 
this stone structure once carried traffic across 
the Willimantic River. Today it offers colorful 
plantings and exquisite views of dams and 
waterfalls. It is convenient to walking, hiking, 
and biking trails, restaurants and other 
amenities.

The Highland Festival Association promotes

dance, piping and harp competitions; a clan

Samuel Slater Experience  
31 Ray St., Webster, MA 01570 
508-461-2955, admin@

The Samuel Slater Experience brings to life 
the American Industrial Revolution with 
immersive, interactive exhibits featuring 
sight, sound and movement. Travel with 
Samuel Slater as he secretly carries spinning 
technology from England to America, builds 
the first successful water-powered textile mill, 
then establishes the mills that made Webster 
a “boomtown. ”

The Woodstock Agricultural Society hosts

Astronomy & Night Sky Views
Explore! OUTDOOR ACTIVITIES
Green by day and dark by night, our National Heritage Corridor is a distinctively dark haven 
amid the urban and suburban glow in the sprawling metropolitan Boston-to-Washington 
corridor. The Last Green Valley has a variety of places to explore dark skies — on your own or 
with TLGV’s Night Sky programs. You can also help TLGV ensure we can continue stargazing 
into the future by becoming a Night Sky Ranger and working with us to reduce light pollution. 
Visit our website to learn more!
Charlton Public Library
40 Main St. (Rt. 31), Charlton, MA
508-248-0452, charltonlibrary.org
The goal of the Library Telescope Program 
is to help foster scientific literacy, stimulate 
an interest in astronomy, and provide 
people who have never looked through 
a telescope the chance to experience the 
excitement that comes from discovery. The 
Telescope was donated to the library by the 
Southbridge Rotary Club and is maintained by 
a partnership with the Aldrich Astronomical 
Society.
Eastern CT State University 
Planetarium
83 Windham St., Willimantic, CT 06226
860-465-5300
easternct.edu/physicalscience/planetarium/
Bring your class, scout troop, office or 
community group on a tour of the Universe. 
See the wonders of our solar system; learn 
about the night sky and more. All shows are 
free of charge. Seating is limited. The dark 
environment may not be suitable for children 
under 7 years old. See details & schedule on 
the website. No fee, but donations accepted.
Girl Scouts of Connecticut  
340 Washington St., Hartford, CT 06106 
800-922-2770
customercare@gsofct.org, gsofct.org 
Visit gsofct.org & see the wide variety of fun 
things we do across Connecticut! In The Last 
Green Valley, Camp Laurel in Lebanon, CT 
is the site for year-round day and overnight 
events for individual girls, troops, and families. 
Gladys E. Kelly  
Public Library Telescope
2 Lake St. (Rt. 12), Webster, MA 01570
508-949-3880, gladyskellylibrary.org
You are encouraged to borrow this 
telescope. The StarBlast 4.5” Astro Reflector 
Telescope was donated through the Aldrich 
Astronomical Society and the tabletop version 
is available for a 1-week conditional loan. 
Experience excitement while viewing various 
celestial bodies. Contact library to learn more.

Sturbridge Trails Heins Farm 
Conservation Area
197 Leadmine Rd., Sturbridge, MA 01566
sturbridgetrails.org
Park in the lot, cross the road and take

be prepared for an amazing surprise when 
you step in and look up. Friends of Sturbridge 
Trails (FrOST) offers programs throughout the 
year, including night hikes during important 
viewing times, such as meteor showers.

Get a different perspective on The Last Green Valley from a bike seat. There are peddling 
opportunities as easy or challenging as you like. Safety is key when biking. Be sure your bike 
is in working order and you are wearing proper attire, including a helmet and highly visible 
clothing. Know what to expect of the terrain, stay hydrated and let someone know where you 
are headed and when you expect to return. For the most up-to-date and detailed information 
on mountain biking, be sure to contact QC NEMBA.
Bicycling & Mountain Biking
Explore! OUTDOOR ACTIVITIES
Air Line State Park Trail
Lebanon, Windham, Chaplin, Hampton,
Pomfret, Putnam, Thompson, CT
860-295-9523 • portal.ct.gov/deep
This linear gravel/pebble covered trail is flat, 
easy and travels for almost 50 miles in The 
Last Green Valley. 3 sections: South from E. 
Hampton to Windham (22 miles), and North 
from Windham to Pomfret (21 miles) plus 
the Thompson section (6.6 miles). The 4-mile 
section from Pomfret to Putnam is currently 
under construction. It is being refurbished 
and regraded and will include new road 
crossings. Originally used from 1873 through 
1955 as a Boston to New York railroad, the 
“Ghost Train” was seen traveling this route 
in earlier days. Now a place to frequent in all 
seasons: hiking, biking, cross-country skiing, 
snowshoeing, jogging, horseback riding 
and even walking your leashed dog. Parking 
locations, section maps, videos and historical 
information available on the website. No fee.
Bigelow Hollow State Park &  
Nipmuck State Forest
Rts. 190 & 171, Union, CT 06076
860-684-3430, 860-424-3200
portal.ct.gov/deep
Advanced Intermediate. Old school technical 
riding, rough and rocky with elevation for 
days. Be prepared to hike a bike depending 
on your choice of routes.
Brimfield State Forest
86 Dearth Hill Rd., Brimfield, MA 01010
413-267-9687,  
mass.gov/locations/brimfield-state-forest
More than 20 miles of roads & trails offer room 
for mountain biking & more! Some fees.

Brimfield – Trolley Trail
120 Sturbridge Rd. (Rt. 20), Brimfield, MA 01010
413-324-8350
info@brimfieldtrail.org, sites.google.com/
view/brimfield-trail-association/home
Approximately 4 miles out & back, this 
trail is open year-round and available for 
walking and bicycling. An old trolley rail bed 
(Springfield to Sturbridge), this is flat & easy 
and is also a section of the Titanic Rail Trail. 
No fee.
East Coast Greenway
greenway.org
2,900 miles total from ME to FL with 198 miles 
thru CT, includes 56 miles in The Last Green 
Valley. Most sections on trails in Coventry 
(Hop River Trail), Windham, Chaplin, Hampton 
and Pomfret (Air Line Trail), Putnam (River 
Trail), Killingly (Quinebaug River Trail), and 
Plainfield (Moosup Valley Trail). Some sections 
on roads in Willimantic, Pomfret, Putnam, 
Killingly, Plainfield and Sterling. Travel through 
varied terrain including old rail trails, historic 
mill towns, forests and farmlands. No fee.
Ekonk Hill Turkey Farm  
227 Ekonk Hill Rd., Sterling/Moosup, CT 06354 
860-564-0248, info@ekonkhillturkeyfarm.com, 
ekonkhillturkeyfarm.com 
Ekonk Hill Turkey Farm is the largest grower 
of pasture raised turkeys in Connecticut. CSA 
sales available Jan. - June. In addition to your 
holiday turkeys, the farm features a full farm 
store and the Brown Cow Café, which includes 
homemade turkey pies, locally raised meats, 
dairy and Ekonk Hill’s signature ice creams, 
scooped March - Nov. In the fall one of the 
region’s largest corn mazes will keep the 
whole family entertained, and homemade 
apple cider donuts (with or without the ice 
cream) can be your sweet treat reward. Ekonk 
Hill Turkey Farm, where tasting is believing! 
Friends of Pachaug Forest, Inc.  
36 Tatro Rd., Griswold, CT 06351 
860-376-5295, friendsofpachaugforest@gmail.
com, friendsofpachaugforest.com 
Pachaug is the largest forest in Connecticut 
with 26,477 acres. This beautiful, bountiful 
forest has offered so much to so many for 
generations. People come from near and 
far to enjoy boating, backpacking, camping, 
cross country skiing, fishing, hiking, horseback 
riding, horse camping, hunting, motorcycling, 
mountain biking, picnicking, scenic vistas, 
snowmobiling and youth group camping. Pets 
can also enjoy the forest if they are leashed.

Hop River State Park Trail
Coventry, Windham, CT
860-424-3200, 
portal.ct.gov/DEEP/State-Parks/Parks/ 
Hop-River-State-Park-Trail
A former railroad line originating to 1849, 
the trail winds from Vernon over 20 miles 
outside The Last Green Valley before entering 
Coventry and Windham. A bridge now 
connects this trail in downtown Willimantic 
to the Air Line State Park Trail for miles of 
recreational possibilities across the National 
Heritage Corridor. Parking locations, section 
maps, historical information and more 
available on the website. No fee.

Nathan Hale State Forest
off Case Rd., Coventry, CT 06238
860-424-3200, portal.ct.gov/deep
Intermediate and up. Miles of single track 
can be explored in these old, historic woods.

North Brookfield Rail Trail
122 Connie Mack Dr., East Brookfield, MA 01515
www.Facebook.com/NorthBrookfield RailTrail/
Park at the East Brookfield Public Library/
Town Hall on Connie Mack Dr. The trail is a 4.2 
mile spur line that runs from the live lines in 
East Brookfield, behind an old shoe factory, 
to the depot in North Brookfield on Elm St. 
Please note the trail is sometimes closed per 
the rail line. Please check the facebook page.

860-923-9440, thompsonct.org/trails-committee
Parking on Rt. 12: at Gazebo, Thompson 
Public Library or behind Knights of Columbus 
Hall. Bike through a historic mill village, along 
the French River and pond. Easy 3.2 miles 
round-trip. Mostly paved, with crushed gravel 
at the end. Gazebo in park hosts summer 
concerts. No fee.
Old Furnace State Park/ 
Ross Pond State Forest
So. Frontage Rd./Ross Rd. (off Rt. 6)
Killingly, CT 06239
860-424-3200, portal.ct.gov/deep
Intermediate and up. Locals will tell you miles 
count as double at Old Furnace. Plenty of 
climbing and tech to get a good workout. 
Great views as well. About 10 miles, but you 
can mix up your loop to get more. 
Pachaug State Forest
Rt. 49, Voluntown, CT 06384
860-376-4075, portal.ct.gov/DEEP/State-
Parks/Forests/Pachaug-State-Forest
With 24,000 acres, CT’s largest forest has 
an exceptional activity selection, including 
mountain biking. Quiet Corner NEMBA has 
worked with CT DEEP to develop mountain 
biking trails. 
Pomfret Forest
118 Wolf Den Rd., Pomfret Center, CT  06259
nemba.org/node/2584
Pomfret Forest is open to hikers, trail runners, 
dog walkers (on leash), mountain bikers and 
equestrians. The 150-acre parcel of land on 
Wolf Den Road near Gwyn Careg Inn was 
recently acquired by the Town of Pomfret, 
which has partnered with QC NEMBA to 
let them build almost 8 miles of trails in a 
wooded setting adjacent to the Air Line Trail 
State Park. Trail users can navigate using  
the TrailForks.com phone app (search for 
Pomfret Forest).
Putnam Quinebaug River Trail/ 
Cargill Falls
Kennedy Dr. & Rt. 44, Putnam, CT 06260
860-963-6800, putnamct.us
Parking lots in a few locations along Kennedy 
Dr. The 1.32-mile Putnam River Trail, flat & 
paved, perfect for new/young pedalers, winds 
through the park & includes a walk along 
the Quinebaug River & past Cargill Falls. 
Interpretive plaques explain area’s history 
with a storybook trail, too. No fee.
Quiet Corner NEMBA  
qcnemba@gmail.com, nemba.org/chapters/
qcnemba, Like us on Facebook! 
NEMBA is a non-profit mountain bike 
advocacy organization with 31 chapters 
in New England. Its mission is to promote 
responsible mountain biking and protect 
and preserve New England trails and open 
spaces. Quiet Corner NEMBA helps maintain 
and develop multi-use trails in northeast 
Connecticut and create riding opportunities 
for all levels. We host weekly group rides,  
offer trail maintenance days and annual  
riding events. 
Quinebaug Lake State Park
59 Shepard Hill Rd., Danielson, CT 06239
portal.ct.gov/deep
A short 2-mile loop around the lake that 
can be combined with the River Trail if you 
venture across Rt. 12. Often flooded during 
high water, plan accordingly.

Quinebaug Valley Rail Trail –  
Perryville Section
Schofield Ave., Dudley, MA 01571
dudleyma.gov/residents/recreation/rail-trail/
Trail head and parking are located off Rt. 12/ 
Schofield Ave near the intersection with New 
Boston Rd. An ongoing rails-to-trails project 
for the discontinued Providence & Worcester 
rail spur from Webster to Southbridge, 
currently this section is 1.5 miles in length 
from Hill St. in Webster to the CT state line. 
Note this section is not contiguous with the 
Southbridge/West Dudley section of the trail. 
No fee.
Quinebaug Valley Rail Trail – 
Southbridge Section
Rt. 131, Southbridge, MA 01550
ci.southbridge.ma.us/385/Quinebaug-Valley-
Rail-Trail
Trail head and parking are located off Rt. 131 
across from the Golden Greek Restaurant. 
An ongoing rails-to-trails project for the 
discontinued Providence & Worcester rail spur 
from Webster to Southbridge. The project 
envisions a connection from Southbridge’s 
Heritage Trail to the onset of the rail trail on 
Crane Street. When completed, it will extend 
11 miles. At this time there are about 5 miles 
completed from Rt. 131 to the Dudley line 
where it connects to the Dudley trail system. 
No fee.
Robbins Preserve –  
Wyndham Land Trust  
Fred Davis Rd. (off Quaddick Town Farm Rd.)
Thompson, CT 06277 • 860-963-2090
info@wyndhamlandtrust.org
wyndhamlandtrust.org
Park at gate. 128-acre diverse tract includes 
forests & meadows and allows mountain 
biking. No motorized vehicles, dogs must be 
leashed. Visit website for map & more details. 
No fee.
UConn Forest – Fenton Tract
Off Storrs Rd. (Rt. 195), Storrs, CT
sustainability.uconn.edu/trails-trees-and-forests
UConn offers many locations for biking & 
hiking. Both the Fenton Tract & the Hillside

are close to campus and offer many different 
routes to explore. Maps available online.  
No fee.
Wells State Park

1,400 wooded acres known for hiking (10 miles

West Thompson Lake
449 Reardon Rd., N. Grosvenordale, CT 06255
860-923-2982
nae.usace.army.mil/Missions/Recreation/ 
West-Thompson-Lake/
Various levels, Cart roads to single track, from

Boating & Fishing
Explore! OUTDOOR ACTIVITIES
Water is the lifeblood of The Last Green Valley  
National Heritage Corridor, and its rivers, streams, 
lakes and ponds offer an incredible variety of boating 
and fishing opportunities. Be sure to read the “State & 
Federal Parks & Forests” and “Paddling” sections and 
visit these websites packed with information to ensure 
every outing is safe and fun. Rules and regulations 
apply and must be followed. No fee unless noted 
otherwise.

Bigelow Hollow State Park
Rt. 171, Union, CT 06076
860-684-3430, 860-424-3200,

Adjoining Nipmuck Forest, 3 bodies of water

vehicles.

Columbia Canoe & Kayak Club at 
Mansfield Hollow State Park  
860-456-4906, baglady@charter.net, 
susanaudette@gmail.com, 
columbiacanoeclub.weebly.com,  
Like us on Facebook! 
A non-profit canoe and kayak club located 
at Mansfield Hollow State Park in Mansfield 
Center, CT. Originally founded on Columbia 
Lake (thus the name) in 1961, we were 
fortunate to make our home at Mansfield 
Hollow in 1989 and built a new clubhouse in 
1998. We have a variety of canoes and kayaks 
in the clubhouse plus accessories. Contact us 
if you are interested in joining - usually new 
members are sponsored by a current member.

Friends of Pachaug Forest, Inc.  
36 Tatro Rd., Griswold, CT 06351 
860-376-5295, friendsofpachaugforest@gmail.
com, friendsofpachaugforest.com 
Pachaug is the largest forest in Connecticut 
with 26,477 acres. This beautiful, bountiful 
forest has offered so much to so many for 
generations. People come from near and 
far to enjoy boating, backpacking, camping, 
cross country skiing, fishing, hiking, horseback 
riding, horse camping, hunting, motorcycling, 
mountain biking, picnicking, scenic vistas, 
snowmobiling and youth group camping. Pets 
can also enjoy the forest if they are leashed.

Local businesses drive the economy of The Last Green Valley. By partnering with business 
groups throughout the corridor we can shine a brighter light on the National Heritage Corridor.

support to help you Explore The Last Green Valley to its fullest potential.

Brooklyn Historical Society Museum & 
Daniel Putnam Tyler Law Office  
25 Canterbury Rd. (Rt. 169),  
Brooklyn, CT 06234 
860-774-7728, brooklynct.org 
Located behind the majestic equestrian 
statue of Revolutionary War hero General 
Israel Putnam (1718 - 1790) is the Brooklyn 
Historical Society Museum. The museum 
houses the permanent General Putnam 
Gallery whose exhibits “Israel Putnam: 
Facets of a Hero” and “General Israel Putnam 
Remembered” explore the fascinating life of 
the farmer and citizen soldier whose daring 
exploits in a Pomfret wolf den and during the 
French and Indian War made him a legend in 
his own day, and the creation of the statue 
that honors him. The Museum’s Special Exhibit 
Gallery features changing displays of local 
history. The current exhibit, “Treasures from 
Brooklyn’s Attic, ” showcases some of the 
varied objects of our collections, items such as 
silver spoons and spectacles made by Edwin 
Newbury (1799 - 1872) in his small shop near 
    F . Kefalas
48

Education
the Brooklyn Green, a Civil War era silk dress, 
ice harvesting equipment, and a circa 1835 
- 1840 oil painting Young Girl with Cat. This 
year there will be an additional display, “The

The museum is wheelchair accessible and 
air conditioned. Behind the museum is the 
19th-century law office of Putnam’s great-
grandson Daniel Putnam Tyler (1798 - 1875) 
furnished as it might have been during his 
career, which included his service as a County 
Court Judge and Secretary of State when he 
supervised Connecticut’s first comprehensive 
economic census. Both museums are open 
May 25 through Oct. 9, every Wed. and Sun. 
from 1 to 5 pm, or by special appointment. 
Please call 860-774-7728 to schedule. There is 
no admission charge for either museum.

Coventry Historical Society

The Coventry Historical Society is a non-
profit group dedicated to discovering, 
collecting and preserving whatever pertains 
to the history of the Town of Coventry and 
promoting historical research in all its aspects 
and making available to the members of the 
Society and to the public, the results of this

Coventry Historical Society –  
Brick School House  
951 Merrow Rd., Coventry, CT 06238 
Built in 1823-25, the school is the center 
of study and research on what school was 
like in the early 20th century up through 
present day schools. Oral histories have been 
collected from people who attended school 
in the one-room schoolhouses in Coventry. 
Many donations of photographs and other 
memorabilia from former students and 
teachers add to the vast collections. The Brick 
School is open to the public on the first and 
third Sunday of the month, May - Oct., from 1 
- 4 pm and on special occasions. Admission is 
free. Donations are gratefully accepted.

Jonathan Trumbull Jr.  
House Museum

860-642-6579, museum@historyoflebanon.org 
historyoflebanon.org 
Tour the elegant 18th century home of Eunice 
and Jonathan Trumbull Jr. Mr. Trumbull 
served as George Washington’s military 
secretary, Speaker of the House and Governor 
of Connecticut. Visitors can select from a 
menu of self-guided tours and talk with our 
history interpreter about the house and its 
residents. Call for open hours or to make an 
appointment. The museum is owned and 
operated by the Lebanon Historical Society. 
Admission is free.

The Samuel Slater Experience brings to life

immersive, interactive exhibits featuring 
sight, sound and movement. Travel with

a “boomtown. ”

PRODUCE SEASONS
With a bevy of farmers markets, farm stands and pick your own locations there are many 
opportunities to get fresh fruits and vegetables throughout The Last Green Valley. The seasons 
can vary from year to year depending on a number of factors, such as weather and the farm.
• Apples (many varieties): As early 
as mid-July, but most September  
through October
• Asparagus: May
• Beans (several varieties): July through 
September
• Beets: June through September
• Blackberries: Mid-July through July
• Blueberries: Mid-July to early-September
• Broccoli: July through September
• Cabbage: Mid-June through October
• Cauliflower: October through November
• Collards: August through October
• Cucumbers: July through August
• Eggplant: Mid-July to mid-September
• Onions: Late-June through July
• Peaches: August to mid-September
• Pears: Mid-August through August
• Peas: July through September
• Peppers: Mid-July through October
• Potatoes: Late-July through September
• Pumpkins: Late-September through 
October
• Raspberries: Early-July through September
• Rhubarb: June through July
• Snap Beans: Late-June to late-July
• Squash: Late-June through early-
September
• Spinach: May through June
• Strawberries: Mid-June
• Sweet Corn: August to late-September
• Tomatoes: Mid-July to mid-September

com, creamerybrookbison.com

Ekonk Hill Turkey Farm  
227 Ekonk Hill Rd., Sterling/Moosup, CT 06354 
860-564-0248, info@ekonkhillturkeyfarm.com, 
ekonkhillturkeyfarm.com 
Ekonk Hill Turkey Farm is the largest grower 
of pasture raised turkeys in Connecticut. CSA 
sales available Jan. - June. In addition to your 
holiday turkeys, the farm features a full farm 
store and the Brown Cow Café, which includes 
homemade turkey pies, locally raised meats, 
dairy and Ekonk Hill’s signature ice creams, 
scooped March - Nov. In the fall one of the 
region’s largest corn mazes will keep the 
whole family entertained, and homemade 
apple cider donuts (with or without the ice 
cream) can be your sweet treat reward. Ekonk 
Hill Turkey Farm, where tasting is believing!

Farmers' Markets
Explore! 
In The Last Green Valley National Heritage Corridor we are lucky to be able to eat and drink 
foods and beverages produced locally. The region’s many Farmers’ Markets connect us to the 
people who create these wonderful products.

Tremko Ln./Pompey Hollow Rd. (Rt. 44), 
Ashford, CT 06278
facebook.com/ashfordfarmersmarketct

Sunday May 8 - Nov. 20 from 10 am - 1 pm at 
Pompey Hollow Park, across the street (Rt. 44) 
from Town Hall. Seasonal produce, fruits, meats, 
cheese, baked goods, syrups, jams and more.

Market – Hitchcock Free Academy  
2 Brookfield Rd./North Main St.,  
Brimfield, MA 01010, hitchcockacademy.org

Oct. 29 (open rain or shine, closed Flea Market 
weeks). Fresh produce, fruits, vegetables, 
herbs, plants, meat, eggs, honey, maple syrup, 
ice cream, baked goods, jams, breads, soaps, 
lotions and crafts. Some vendors accept EBT-
SNAP , HIP & coupons.

The Coventry Farmers’ Market is dedicated 
to providing the most vibrant, community 
minded market possible. Open every Sunday 
from June – Oct., our market carries the 
tradition of being the largest farmers’ market 
in Connecticut, featuring some of the best of 
CT-grown, CT-made products around at one 
of the most unspoiled countryside settings in 
the state.

Hiking, Walking & Running Trails
Explore! OUTDOOR ACTIVITIES
MUL TI-TOWN
Air Line State Park Trail 
Lebanon, Windham, Chaplin, Hampton, 
Pomfret, Putnam, Thompson, CT
860-295-9523, portal.ct.gov/deep
This linear gravel/pebble covered trail is 
flat, easy and travels for almost 50 miles 
in The Last Green Valley. 3 sections: South 
from E. Hampton to Windham (22 miles), 
North from Windham to Pomfret (21 miles), 
and the Thompson section (6.6 miles). The 
4-mile section from Pomfret to Putnam is 
almost complete and has new road crossings. 
Additional refurbishing and regrading is 
ongoing. Putnam is working on connecting 
the trail through town to Thompson. 
Originally used from 1873 through 1955 as a 
Boston to New York railroad, the “Ghost Train” 
was seen traveling this route in earlier days. 
Now a place to frequent in all seasons: hiking, 
biking, cross-country skiing, snowshoeing, 
jogging, horseback riding and even walking 
your leashed dog. Parking locations, section 
maps, videos and historical information 
available on the website. Business-card sized 
z-maps for many of sections of the trail are 
available with each town and at The Last 
Green Valley office. Mobility reference guides 
are also available at TheLastGreenValley.org. 
No fee. 
Category Sponsored by Savers Bank
You can hike, walk and run your way around the National Heritage Corridor. There are more 
than 500 miles of trails, from those suited for the most ambitious hikers and runners to wooded 
paths for the most relaxed strollers. This list is only a sample of what you can find. Enjoy guided 
adventures offered by TLGV every month and join us and our partners during Spring Outdoors 
from March through June, and Walktober each fall. To help you explore more of The Last Green 
Valley we’ve organized this section by town; listed first in this section are the trails that cross 
town borders, as well as our partners who have land or support public lands in multiple towns. 
    F . Kefalas

Grand Trunk Trail/Titanic Rail Trail
Brimfield, Southbridge, Sturbridge, MA
sturbridgetrails.org/grand-trunk-trail.html
Brimfield: 2 parking lots, 1 on the south side 
of Rt. 20 (0.5 miles east of downtown) & the 
other on the south side of Five Bridge Rd. 
Southbridge: parking at Marjorie Lane & in 
Westville Lake Recreation Area. Sturbridge: 
parking at Wallace Rd. This hard-packed trail 
with some incomplete sections will stretch 
more than 60 miles, and travels through 
Brimfield, Sturbridge, and Southbridge, 
meeting up with the Air Line Trail in 
Thompson. You’ll find other trail connections 
and spurs off this trail too. No fee.

Hop River State Park Trail
Coventry, Windham, CT
860-424-3200, portal.ct.gov/deep
A former railroad line dating back to 1849, 
the trail winds from Vernon over 20 miles 
outside The Last Green Valley before entering 
Coventry and Windham. A new bridge now 
connects this trail in downtown Willimantic 
to the Air Line State Park Trail for miles of 
recreational possibilities across the National 
Heritage Corridor. Parking locations, section 
maps, historical information and more 
available on the website. No fee.

Joshua’s Trust is a non-profit land 
conservation trust protecting 5,000 acres in 
Northeastern Connecticut that will remain 
open space and protected from development 
forever. Joshua’s Trust works in fourteen 
towns in Windham and Tolland counties and 
cooperates with other land trusts beyond 
this region. We maintain 40 miles of walking 
trails for the public to appreciate and enjoy, 
and two sites of historic significance: the 
Gurleyville Grist Mill and Atwood Farm in 
Mansfield. Explore our properties, learn more 
about what we do and find out how to get 
involved! We hope to see you out on the  
trails soon!

Sprague Land Preserve  
(aka Mukluk Preserve)
Franklin & Sprague
Holton Rd., Franklin, CT 06254
860-822-3000, ctsprague.org
Take dirt road on right off Holton Rd and park 
at cabin, or park in Watson lot on left at end 
of Holton Rd. Formerly known as the Mukluk 
Preserve, the Town of Sprague purchased 
this property in 2005 with the help of a land 
acquisition grant from CT DEEP . Take in the 
beautiful setting, many wooded trails and 
area wildlife. Just below the Scotland Dam on 
the Shetucket River, enjoy 1 mile of riverfront. 
See website for trail map. Property has trails in 
both Franklin and Sprague. Hunting is allowed 
on the property, so take precautions. No fee.
Tackle the Trail  
Northeast Connecticut 
Monique Wolanin, Race Director 
tacklethetrail.org 
Tackle the Trail, a unique race in an amazing 
place! Always the 3rd Saturday in Oct., choose 
from Marathon, ½ Marathon and Relay team 
options. Runners enjoy the quiet solitude of 
nature with bursts of energetic and supportive 
fans throughout the course, which offers both 
technical and non-technical sections of trail 
through Windham, Pomfret and Putnam, CT. 
Highlights include amazing scenery, great 
swag, enthusiastic volunteers and fun post-
race festivities. Proceeds from this race support 
students pursuing their educational dreams 
at Quinebaug Valley Community College. All 
race information can be found at tacklethetrail.
org. Race does sell-out. Register now and make 
Tackle your destination! 
TriTown Forest Preserve –  
Avalonia Land Conservancy  
Griswold, Preston, North Stonington
90 Miller Rd., North Stonington, CT 06359
860-884-3500, avalonia.org
This 527-acre property was preserved in 2018 
by Avalonia Land Conservancy and has land in 
Preston, Griswold, and North Stonington. The 
best parking location is off Miller Rd. in North 
Stonington. There are numerous trail loops of 
varying difficulty; this peaceful property has 
9.4 total miles of trails. Significant historical 
stone walls. All trails are well marked, dogs are 
allowed but must be leashed. Benches allow 
for scenic views and tranquil resting areas. 
No fee.
Wyndham Land Trust, Inc.  
860-963-2090, info@wyndhamlandtrust.org,

The mission of the Wyndham Land Trust is to 
conserve the natural resources of Northeast 
Connecticut - the water courses, swamps, 
woodlands and open spaces, the plant and 
animal life therein, and the scenic natural 
and historic sites - all of which are very much 
a part of the character and beauty of all 
communities in Northeast Connecticut. Our 
work is urgent.

Quinebaug Valley Rail Trail -  
Dudley Section
Mill Rd., Dudley, MA 01571
508-949-8000, dudleyma.gov
Park in lot on Mill Rd. in West Dudley. This 
trail runs 6.12 miles along the Quinebaug 
River, and under your feet is the old rail bed 
originally surveyed for the Southbridge & 
Blackstone Railroad in 1831. The trail also runs 
from Southbridge for another 0.53 miles to 
the CT border. No fee.
Quinebaug Valley Rail Trail –  
Perryville Section
Schofield Ave, Dudley, MA 01571
dudleyma.gov/recreation/rail-trail

for the discontinued Providence & Worcester 
rail spur from Webster to Southbridge, 
currently this section is 1.5 miles in length 
from Hill St. in Webster to the CT state line. 
Note this section is not contiguous with the 
Southbridge/West Dudley section of the trail. 
No fee.
Tufts Branch Valley Wildlife Sanctuary & 
Keekamoochaug Wildlife Sanctuary – 
Dudley Conservation Land Trust  
Healy Rd., Dudley, MA 01571
508-949-2468, dudleyclt.org

EAST BROOKFIELD 
East Brookfield Town Common
Depot Square (Mechanic & Pleasant Sts.), East 
Brookfield, MA 01515
508-867-6769, eastbrookfieldma.us
Park on street. Within a section of town long 
known as “Podunk, ” Vizard’s Common was 
given by a private citizen in 1931 to the new 
town with the stipulation that a World War I 
memorial be placed there. More monuments 
for World War II and the Korean & Vietnam 
conflicts were added. The Common is 
surrounded by the former town hall, library, 
senior center, general store and various 19th-
century structures. A short stroll. No fee. 
North Brookfield Rail Trail 
122 Connie Mack Dr., East Brookfield, MA 01515
https://www.facebook.com/
NorthBrookfieldRailTrail/
Park at the East Brookfield Public Library/
Town Hall on Connie Mack Dr. The trail is a 4.2 
mile spur line that runs from the live lines in 
East Brookfield, behind an old shoe factory, 
to the depot in North Brookfield on Elm St. 
Please note the trail is sometimes closed per 
the rail line. Please check the Facebook page.

HOLLAND
Lake Siog Recreation Area/ 
Lake Siog Pass
off Dug Hill Rd., Holland, MA 01521

Quinebaug Woods
Dug Hill Road, Holland, MA 01521
413-245-7108, town.holland.ma.us
Moderate/short 1.1-mile loop trail goes along 
the Quinebaug River then heads up and over 
a steep hillside with rocky outcrops. Mixed 
open canopy along the top of the “hogback” 
ridge, hemlocks dominate below, forming 
dense stands. View remains from a cabin built 
in 1932. Look over Blake Hill to the east and 
Hamilton Reservoir to the south. Year-round 
habitat varies throughout supporting spotted 
salamanders, beavers, kingfishers, herons, 
seasonal blooms & more. No fee.
OXFORD 
Hodges Village Dam
30 Howarth Rd., Oxford, MA 01540
508-248-5697, nae.usace.army.mil/Missions/
Recreation/Hodges-Village-Dam/
Park on top of & below dam, at the end of 
Rocky Hill Rd., at Greenbriar Park & at Multiple 
Use Area off Clara Barton Rd. in North Oxford. 
Part of a system of 6 flood risk management 
projects designed and built in the Thames 
River Basin by the U.S. Army Corps, Hodges 
Village Dam is a dry bed reservoir, now 
with approximately 22 miles of year-round 
recreational trails. MA-registered off-highway 
motorcycles are allowed on the west side of 
the French River only. Some fees.
SOUTHBRIDGE
Claire Birtz Wildlife Sanctuary – 
Opacum Land Trust  
Tipton Rock Rd. (off Rt. 169),  
Southbridge, MA 01550
508-347-9144, info@opacumlt.org, opacumlt.org
Small parking lot on Tipton Rock Rd. 3 trails 
available (0.64, 0.5 and 0.41 miles) that wind 
along the shore of Morse Pond, through 
stands of beech trees, hemlock, and mixed 
forest habitats. There are old stone walls. 
History and nature at the site are inspiring. 
No fee.
Heritage Trail – Southbridge
West St., Southbridge, MA 01550
ci.southbridge.ma.us/southbridge-trail-
committee
Parking at West Street School. An 800-yard 
ADA-compliant trail running parallel to the 
Quinebaug River with benches, boardwalks 
with railings, a scenic river overlook & picnic 
area. The school offers baseball & soccer fields, 
tennis courts & a playground. The Heritage 
Trail leads west to the Westville Dam & more 
trails: the Heritage Trail Extension, Grand 
Trunk Trail and Heritage Connector Bridge 
(over the dam spillway) form a 1.7-mile loop 
which circles Westville Lake. Continue west 
along the Grand Trunk Trail to the Ed Calcutt 
Bridge for 1.3 miles more. No fee.
Quinebaug Valley Rail Trail – 
Southbridge Section
Rt. 131, Southbridge, MA 01550
ci.southbridge.ma.us/385/Quinebaug-Valley-
Rail-Trail
Trail head and parking are located off Rt. 131 
across from the Golden Greek Restaurant. 
An ongoing rails-to-trails project for the 
discontinued Providence & Worcester rail spur 
from Webster to Southbridge. The project 
envisions a connection from Southbridge’s 
Heritage Trail to the onset of the rail trail on 
Crane Street. When completed, it will extend 
11 miles. At this time there are about 5 miles 
completed from Rt. 131 to the Dudley line 
where it connects to the Dudley trail system. 
No fee.
Thompson Family “Forever Wild” 
Preserve Loop Trail – Opacum  
Land Trust  
Eastford Rd. (Rt. 198), just south of Dennison 
Dr., Southbridge, MA 01550
508-347-9144, opacumlt.org
40-acre preserve is adjacent to almost 2,300 
acres of watershed land owned by the town. 
1-mile loop begins at a small overgrown 
pasture, crosses wetlands, access roads and 
old logging road, back down hill to trailhead. 
Complete map online. No fee.

visitsturbridge.org
A 0.4-mile ADA-compliant trail starting and 
ending at the lower Burgess School parking 
lot. The trail passes along the ball field into an 
upland and wetland forest cover environment 
traveling over some 150-ft. of wetland 
(bog bridge), passes by several large glacial 
boulders and around a gravel drumlin upland 
area. Trail access information is posted on the 
trail sign. As this is school property, all school 
property access rules apply. Contact the 
school office at 508-347-7041 with  
any questions.

24 Riverview Ave., (off Rt. 20), Fiskdale

508-347-3705, nae.usace.army.mil/Missions/

ASHFORD

Spur to Ruby Fenton Preserve
off Lustig Rd., Ashford, CT 06278
860-487-4400, ashfordtownhall.org/directory/

Creamery Brook Trail

The Milo Appley Conservation 
Showcase – ECCD  
139 Wolf Den Rd., Brooklyn, CT 06234

Eastern CT Conservation District owns 85 acres

White Brook Sanctuary - Eastern CT 
Forest Landowners Assoc./Wolf Den 
Land Trust  
Darby Rd., Brooklyn, CT 06234, ecfla.org
Park on road. Easy 0.8-mile loop trail. Beware 
of traffic. Sanctuary skirts the edges of both 
a forested and sedge swamp with numerous

species. The area is transected by White Brook 
and at one point follows along the top of a 
dike near a small pond. No fee.
CANTERBURY
Canterbury Town Green
Jct. Rts. 169 & 14, Canterbury, CT 06331
860-546-7800, canterburyct.org
1.5-mile easy loop, beware of traffic. A 
National Historic District with more than 
32 varied architecturally and historically 
significant properties dating from 1709 
to 1747, including the Prudence Crandall 
Museum. Rt. 14, the Revolutionary route of 
the French Army under Rochambeau from 
Newport to Yorktown, crosses through the 
Canterbury Green. No fee.
CHAPLIN
Two Sisters Tract - Joshua’s Trust  
Rt. 6 (Rt. 203 intersection), Chaplin, CT 06235
860-429-9023, joshuastrust.org
Park on Rt. 6 shoulder, north of Rts. 6/203 
intersection. 2 parcels, totaling 40 acres, split 
by the Air Line Trail. Path from Rt. 6 goes to 
Air Line. Cross the rail bed & a steep staircase 
leads to 1-mile loop trail on the larger parcel. 
For longer walk, park at Rt. 203, walk north 
along the Air Line 0.86 miles to Two Sisters 
loop & back for total of 2.6 miles. See website 
for map. No fee.
 
 
COVENTRY
Creaser Park Nature Trail
Case Rd., Coventry, CT 06238
860-742-4068, coventryct.org
2 miles easy to moderate round-trip with 
limited handicapped access to pond. Trail 
leads across meadow, along the Skunkamug 
River, around pond and through varied forest 
terrain. Search for more than 40 species of 
birds. Leashed dogs welcome. No fee.
Nathan Hale State Forest
off Case Rd., Coventry, CT 06238
860-424-3200, portal.ct.gov/deep
1,500 acres of forest and fields bordered by 
roads offer hiking or strolling, but no trails are 
maintained. A great place to hone compass 
skills and stumble upon ruins. Historic Nathan 
Hale Homestead is close by to extend your 
visit. Wildlife viewing is ideal. Hunting is 
allowed in season. No fee
EASTFORD
Boston Turnpike Trail & Greenway
Rt. 198/John Perry Rd., Eastford, CT 06242
860-974-1885, eastfordconservation.org/
trails/trailsindex.html
Park in unpaved area at Rt. 198 adjacent to 
John Perry Rd. Trail also accessible off Old 
Colony Rd. Stand on the ONLY remaining 
unaltered portion of an early road system that 
first connected cities & towns during colonial 
times. Established under King Charles II of 
Britain, the roads followed trails laid out much 
earlier by Native Americans and were used 
mainly for carrying the mail. Farmers also used 
the roads to get their goods to markets, and 
they were vital for moving troops during the 
American Revolution. Pass through Natchaug 
State Forest and over privately-owned land, 
whose owners who have given permission for 
the public to use this trail. Be respectful. The 
portion of the trail between Old Colony Rd. 
and State Forest Rd. passes through a typical 
maple/oak/hickory forest. Near State Forest 
Rd., pass through wetland habitat. A small 
bridge & short length of boardwalk have been 
constructed to ease your passage through 
these areas. See website for more information. 
No fee.

George Askew Nature Trail
12 Westford Rd., Eastford, CT 06242
860-974-0125, eastfordct.org
Park in the Elementary School parking lot. 
This short & easy nature trail (approximately 1

FRANKLIN
Bailey’s Ravine at Ayer Gap
291 Pond Rd. (Rt. 207), Franklin, CT 06254
203-568-6270, nature.org
This 80-acre property owned by The Nature

No fee.

LEBANON
Lebanon Town Green Loop
Jct. Rts. 87 & 207, Lebanon, CT 06249
860-642-6579, historyoflebanon.org
Park at the Lebanon Historical Society & 
Visitors Center, 286 Rt. 87. Easy 2-mile loop 
walk around historic 1-mile town green. A 
National Register Historic District that was 
of great importance during the American 
Revolution. Visit where Gen. George 
Washington reviewed his troops in 1781, see 
30+ historic buildings & sites, including the 
War Office where Washington, Rochambeau & 
CT Gov. Jonathan Trumbull (the only colonial 
governor to support the Revolution) met.  
No fee.

860-424-3200, portal.ct.gov/deep
This 577-acre park was a former boys camp.

returning to nature, as well remains of the 
300-year-old Granny Austin Tavern as you 
hike. No fee.
Pomeroy State Park
Rt. 289, Lebanon, CT 06249
860-424-3200, portal.ct.gov/deep
This undeveloped and unmarked 200-acre

The John Hibbard Trail, named in honor of

property and acts as a forestry educational 
resource for all ages. Recently harvested to 
help regenerate oak and pitch pine species 
and to improve the site for wildlife. No fee.

MANSFIELD/STORRS
Albert E. Moss Wildlife Sanctuary

860-429-3015, preserves@mansfieldct.org,

Park at Town Hall/Community Center. Cross

220 wooded acres owned partly by the town

Fifty-Foot Cliff Preserve
954 Storrs Rd. (Rt. 195), Mansfield, CT 06250
860-429-3015, preserves@mansfieldct.org, 
mansfieldct.gov
Park behind Historical Society. 102 acres of 
mostly forested land with an easy 1.41-mile

for topographic accuracy, the cliff is actually 
100 feet high. Enjoy clifftop views of the 
Fenton River Valley, Lake Naubesatuck and the 
distant ridges of Scotland. Map with points of 
interest online. No fee.

Mansfield Hollow State Park
Bassetts Bridge Rd., Mansfield, CT 06250
860-928-6121, 860-424-3200, portal.ct.gov/deep
Lots of trails to explore. Easy 1.5-mile loop 
red-blazed Eagle Trail was completed as an 
Eagle Scout project. Through woodlands, old 
farm roads, along the reservoir and on old cart 
paths. Great views of lake too. No fee.
Merrow Meadow Park
Merrow Rd., Mansfield, CT 06268
860-429-3015, preserves@mansfieldct.org, 
mansfieldct.gov
Easy 0.57-mile loop trail follows the east side 
of the Willimantic River, with interpretive 
signs, accessible fishing pad (0.32 miles are 
paved, 0.25 miles are gravel). No fee.
UConn Forest – Fenton Tract
off Storrs Rd. (Rt. 195), Storrs, CT
ecohusky.uconn.edu/trails-trees-and-forests
UConn offers many locations for biking & 
hiking. Both the Fenton Tract & the Hillside 
Environmental Education Park (HEEP) Trails 
are close to campus and offer many different 
routes to explore. You can also discover many 
varieties of trees on campus through the 
Campus Tree Touring Guide. Maps available 
online. No fee.
