Hiking, Walking & Running Trails
UConn Horsebarn Hill
Horsebarn Hill Rd., Storrs, CT 06269
860-486-2413, animalscience.uconn.edu
Easy 1.1-mile accessible walk with animal 
barns open to the public. See newborn lambs 
in spring. Fields of pigs, horses, cattle & sheep. 
Dairy Bar with famous UConn ice cream is 
nearby! No fee for self-guided tours.
Whetten Woods – Joshua’s Trust  
62 Dog Ln., Storrs, CT 06268
860-429-9023, joshuastrust.org
Park at the Hope Lutheran Church. Trail is 
behind church. Although there is no direct 
road access to this preserve, you’ll find 
a Joshua’s Trust sign & a connecting trail 
through the church property. 1.5 miles on 
the yellow-blazed trail. The view on this 
37-acre property is mixed hardwood forest 
with upland, stream and wetland. Many stone 
walls. An easy walk with some rooted areas. 
See website for map. No fee.
Wolf Rock Nature Preserve –  
Joshua’s Trust  
Crane Hill Rd., Mansfield, CT 06268
860-429-9023, joshuastrust.org
One of Mansfield’s most spectacular landmarks, 
a 6-foot diameter glacial boulder sits atop a 
sheer 40-foot cliff. Wolves may have inhabited 
the area when the town was settled, but by 
the turn of this century this location became 
a great place for a walk. From the top of the 
ledge you can see the Sawmill Brook Valley 
below and fields on Crane Hill Rd. No fee.
NORWICH
Benedict Arnold Trail –  
Walk Norwich  
Corner of Washington St. & Arnold Pl. 
Norwich, CT 06360
860-886-1776, info@walknorwich.org, 
walknorwich.org
Park at Leffingwell House Museum, 348 
Washington St., or at the Norwich Heritage 
& Regional Visitors’ Center, 69 East Town St. 
One of the world’s most notorious traitors 
called Norwich home. Discover the story of 
a controversial and complicated Benedict 
Arnold, who greatly impacted our nation’s 
history during the Revolutionary War. The 
self-guided tour gives a glimpse into Benedict 
Arnold’s complex childhood and discusses 
prominent Norwich figures who played a 
pivotal role in the Revolutionary War. Walk 
2 easy miles (some uneven terrain) past the 
family homestead, Leffingwell Inn, Olmstead-
Lathrop Manor (from the 1600s) and more. 
Maps & more available on website. No fee.
Ellis Walter Ruley Memorial Park
28 Hammond Ave., Norwich, CT 06360
walknorwich.org/freedom-trail/stop-15/
Part of the CT Freedom Trail and the Norwich 
Freedom Trail, this park features a short but 
steep walk up to the homestead of artist Ellis 
Walter Ruley. You’ll find interpretive panels, 
the remains of Ruley’s home and a place to sit 
and contemplate his story. 
Heritage Trail – Walk Norwich  
100 Chelsea Harbor Dr., Norwich, CT 06360
860-886-1776, walknorwich.org
Park at Howard T. Brown Memorial Park. 
This riverfront trail along the Yantic River 
and Norwich Harbor includes historic 
and interpretive signs describing Chelsea 
Landing. The trail follows city streets for 
short segments. Walk upstream starting from 
Howard T. Brown Park on Norwich Harbor at 
Chelsea Harbor Drive to Yantic Falls. Some 
sections are handicap-accessible. Leashed 
dogs are welcome. No fee.
The Norwich Heritage and  
Regional Visitors’ Center  
69 East Town St., Norwich, CT 06360
860-886-1776, Fax: 860-886-0452
info@norwichhistoricalsociety.org, 
walknorwich.org
One of the locations to get your National  
Park Service Passport stamped in The Last  
Green Valley
Mohegan Park
Park Center Rd./Mohegan Park Rd., 
Norwich, CT 06360
860-823-3798, norwichct.org
Park includes 3 trails (0.7 loop, plus 0.4 & 0.6 
milers). Beautiful Norwich Rose Garden is
located on Rockwell St. in Mohegan Park, on 2 
acres of gently sloping parkland & features
2,500 rose bushes in 120 varieties. 
Playgrounds and more! No fees.

Hiking, Walking & Running Trails
Uncas Leap Walking Trail – Walk 
Norwich
134 Yantic St., Norwich, CT 06360
860-886-1776, info@walknorwich.org, 
walknorwich.org
Park at Uncas Leap at 134 Yantic St. An easy 
2-mile self-guided walk features the Native 
Mohegan Tribe’s rich history in southeastern 
CT, including a legendary battle with the 
Narragansett Tribe. Along the way you’ll see 
stunning Yantic Falls, the Uncas monument, 
Royal Mohegan Burial Grounds and the 
Mohegan Landing. Map & more available on 
website. No fee.
PLAINFIELD 
Fitze Preserve – Wyndham  
Land Trust   
Plainfield Recreation/Senior Center
482 Norwich Rd. (Rt. 12), Plainfield, CT 06374
860-963-2090, info@wyndhamlandtrust.org
wyndhamlandtrust.org
Park behind Rec./Senior Center. 3.5-acre 
wooded lot offers a Children’s Discovery 
Trail that was created to allow youngsters 
a chance to explore the trees and shrubs in 
typical Northeast Connecticut woods. The 
stone walls crisscrossing the property remind 
us this was once open farmland. The trail 
is an easy 20-minute hike. Strollers are not 
recommended. No fee.
Moosup Valley State Park Trail
Jct. Main St. & Rt. 14, Plainfield, CT 06354
portal.ct.gov/DEEP/State-Parks/Parks/
Moosup-Valley-State-Park-Trail
A 5.8-mile linear trail runs to Sterling with 
connection to Rhode Island. A former rail 
trail, you’ll traverse gravel to ballast surfaces. 
Glimpse the Moosup River & Sterling Pond in 
this rural setting. Good for beginners. No fee.
Quinebaug Valley State Trout 
Hatchery/Trolley Trail
141 Trout Hatchery Rd.,  
Central Village, CT 06332
860-564-7542, portal.ct.gov/deep
Easy 0.7-mile walk starts at the hatchery, 
follows the Trolley Trail along abandoned 
trolley track to Plainfield’s Wauregan National 
Historic Register District, a 19th-century mill 
village and c. 1850 H-shaped stone cotton 
mill. No fee.
POMFRET
Aicher Preserve –  
Wyndham Land Trust  
Freedley Rd., Pomfret, CT 06259
860-963-2090, info@wyndhamlandtrust.org, 
wyndhamlandtrust.org
Look for WLT sign, park on side of road, enter 
through farm gate. Cornfield is on left, a lake 
on right as you proceed. Turn right into a 
network of woods roads or continue straight 
ahead and over the dam. Follow the tree line 
to either the right or the left. From the high 
point of the preserve, elevation 505’ , you have 
striking views of the surrounding countryside. 
Below are a splendid hayfield, cornfields, 
and woodlands. Excellent bird habitat. Visit 
website for map & more details. No fee.
Connecticut Audubon Society  
Center at Pomfret  
218 Day Rd., Pomfret Center, CT 06259 
860-928-4948, ctaudubon.org/pomfret-home 
Duck Marsh Preserve –  
Wyndham Land Trust  
Wrights Crossing Rd. or Holmes Rd.,  
Pomfret, CT 06259
860-963-2090, info@wyndhamlandtrust.org, 
wyndhamlandtrust.org
Parking at both road entrances. Look for 
Wyndham Land Trust signs. 146-acre wetland 
habitat with access from Wrights Crossing Rd. 
on the north & Holmes Rd. on the south. Ideal 
location for bird watching, with 2 blinds and 2 
observation platforms. No motorized vehicles 
allowed. Dogs must be leashed. Visit website 
for map & more details. No fee.
Grassland Bird Conservation Center at 
Pomfret – CT Audubon Society  
218 Day Rd., Pomfret Center, CT 06259
860-928-4948, ctaudubon.org/center- 
at-pomfret
This 702-acre nature sanctuary is a nationally 
recognized important bird area and includes 
a large beaver pond, acres of grasslands, 
streams and a hemlock ravine. More than 
10 miles of walking trails with interpretive 
signage. Environmental programming year-
round. No fees for self-guided hikes, and 
guided hikes are offered for member & non-
member prices.

Kimball Memorial Forest –  
New England Forestry Foundation  
(NEFF)  
Wolf Den Rd., Pomfret, CT 06259
978-952-6856, newenglandforestry.org
This 167-acre property is located on the west 
side of Wolf Den Rd., 0.5 mile south of the

state forest land, and consists of a variety 
of oaks with pine, hemlock and hardwoods

Lyon Preserve –  
Wyndham Land Trust  
33 Wrights Crossing Rd., Pomfret, CT 06259
860-963-2090, info@wyndhamlandtrust.org, 
wyndhamlandtrust.org
Park in the preserve. Look for WLT sign at 
entrance. 46-acre preserve with 2 portions 
— an upland tract characterized by early 
succession vegetation & lowlands bordering 
Mashamoquet Brook. A well-maintained trail 
system meanders through birding habitat 
with confirmed sightings of many varieties. 
Dogs must be leashed. Visit website for map & 
more details. No fee.
Mashamoquet Brook State Park/ 
Wolf Den
147 Wolf Den Dr. (Rt. 44),  
Pomfret Center, CT 06259
860-928-6121, portal.ct.gov/DEEP/State-
Parks/Parks/Mashamoquet-Brook-State-Park
Easy to moderate 4.75-mile loop trail includes 
some steep hills up to the Wolf’s Den, on the 
National Register of Historic Places, where 
Revolutionary War hero Israel Putnam killed 
the last wolf in CT according to folklore. Also 
see “Indian Chair” rock formation on top of a 
20’ cliff overlooking the valley. No fee.
Pomfret Rec. Park Trail
576 Hampton Rd. (Rt. 97),  
Pomfret Center, CT 06259
860-974-1423, pomfretct.gov
Enjoy a walk through the woods starting 
at the trailhead by the ball fields. This trail 
connects to the Air Line Trail for expanded 
walking fun. No fee.
PRESTON 
Preston Community Park Nature Trail
13 Rt. 117, Preston, CT 06365
860-887-5581, preston-ct.org
Easy 0.3-mile handicapped-accessible, family/
children friendly loop trail with interpretive
signs. Hilly but not strenuous, with scenic 
overlooks with benches. Near playscape & 
picnic area. Connects with Blue-Blazed Pequot 
Trail. No fee.
Preston Nature Preserve – Avalonia 
Land Conservancy   
South side of Krug Rd., 0.6 miles west of 
Jewett City Rd., Preston, CT 06365,  
860-884-3500, avalonialc@yahoo.com, 
avalonialandconservancy.org
Two trails featuring varied land features 
and vegetation, including two hills, a valley, 
hardwood and cedar forest, brushland, 
meadows, pastures, swamps and ponds. Well-
established 0.5-mile trail system with bridges 
and Hike and Seek opportunity.
PUTNAM
Putnam Quinebaug River Trail/ 
Cargill Falls
Kennedy Dr. & Rt. 44, Putnam, CT 06260
860-963-6800, putnamct.us
Park in a few lots on Kennedy Dr. With the 
extension added in 2016, the Putnam River 
Trail is over 2.5 miles long – one way! Flat & 
paved, the trail winds through Rotary and 
Simonzi Parks and includes a walk along 
the Quinebaug River & past Cargill Falls. 
Interpretive plaques explain the area’s history 
while storybook trail entertains the kids.  
No fee.
SCOTLAND
Mohegan State Forest
Waldo Rd. (off Rt. 97), Scotland, CT 06264
860-424-3200, portal.ct.gov/deep
700 acres in 2 parcels (smaller piece in 
Sprague) make up this forest of hardwood 
and softwood. Roadside parking by gate (no 
parking area), will lead you to gravel road for 
hiking serenity. No fee

Pappenheimer Preserve –  
Joshua’s Trust

Rock Spring Preserve
Pudding Hill Rd. (Rt. 97), Scotland, CT 06247
203-568-6270, nature.org
The 450-acre Nature Conservancy property 
offers a 3.5 mile loop trail along the Little 
River, as well as a cut-through trail on an old 
farm access road. There is a great east-facing 
scenic vista looking over the valley into 
Canterbury, as well as a natural spring. Some 
sections of the trail along the river may be 
more overgrown. Park along the road. No fee.

THOMPSON
Bull Hill Recreation Area
Messier Rd. (off Ravenelle Rd.) N.

Park at Messier Rd. gate or next to soccer field.

Fisherman’s Walk Trail
Fabyan-Woodstock Rd., N.  
Grosvenordale, CT 06255
860-923-9440, thompsonct.org/trails-

Park at trailhead on Fabyan-Woodstock Rd. 
Easy 1-mile round-trip (about 30 minutes)

bend for 1.2 miles total. Car-top boat launch 
for National Recreation Water Trail ending in 
West Thompson Lake. No fee.
North Grosvenordale River Walk/Park
Riverside Dr. (Rt. 12),  
N. Grosvenordale, CT 06255
860-923-9440, thompsonct.org/trails-committee
Park on Rt. 12 at Gazebo, Public Library, or 
behind Knights of Columbus. Walk through 
historic mill village, along French River and 
pond. It’s an easy 3.2-mile roundtrip (1 hour 
20 minutes). Mostly paved, with crushed 
gravel at the end. Mobility & bike accessible. 
No fee.
Quaddick State Park Red Loop Trail
818 Town Farm Rd., Thompson, CT 06277
860-928-6121, 860-424-3200, portal.ct.gov/deep
In a park well known for its water activities, be 
sure to take an easy hike on the 1.54-mile loop
trail through forested areas, which also 
crosses Quaddick Town Farm Rd. Some fees.

Robbins Preserve –  
Wyndham Land Trust  
Fred Davis Rd. (off Quaddick Town Farm Rd.)
Thompson, CT 06277, 860-963-2090
info@wyndhamlandtrust.org,

Park at gate. 128-acre diverse tract, a former

Thompson Common
Intersection Rts. 200 & 193, Thompson, CT 06277
860-923-3776, thompsonhistorical.org
Beautiful all year, the Thompson Common is a

stroll over to the Ellen Larned Museum and

Tri-State Marker
E. Thompson Rd. & New Rd., Thompson, CT 06277
860-923-9440, thompsonct.org/trails-committee
2-mile round-trip (1 hour) Blue-Blazed Trail 
starts on flat Air Line Trail, to CT/MA stone 
border marker on right, then challenging 
uphill, rocky climb to historic tri-state marker 
erected in 1883. Off-shoot trails into RI & MA, 
connecting to Mid-State Trail & Douglas State 
Forest Loop Trail. No fee.
West Thompson Lake Shoreline Trail
449 Reardon Rd., N. Grosvenordale, CT 06255
860-923-2982, nae.usace.army.mil/Missions/
Recreation/West-Thompson-Lake/
Easy 4+ mile loop trail starts to right of boat 
ramp. Follow West Thompson Lake’s shoreline
through meadows to mature forest. The corps 
has completed the crossing to Blain Rd. with a 
new bridge. No fee.
UNION
Bigelow Hollow State Park 
Rts. 190 & 171, Union, CT 06076
860-684-3430, 860-424-3200, portal.ct.gov/deep
You’ll find easy to moderate and difficult 
hiking here depending on season and 
conditions. For a short, easy hike consider the 
Bigelow Pond Loop. If you want a challenge, 
Mashapaug Lake offers a hike of more than 10 
miles. Expect water crossings. Keep your eyes 
on the skies and the tree line to spot the pair 
of bald eagles calling the lake home. 
VOLUNTOWN
Pachaug State Forest/ 
Green Falls Pond Trail
Rt. 49, Voluntown, CT 06384
860-376-4075, portal.ct.gov/deep
Easy to moderate 2-mile loop trail beginning 
at either end of the camping and picnic area 
is blue-blazed with red mark. Goes around 
Green Falls Pond and down Green Falls Brook. 
If you lose your bearings, follow lake shore 
until you pick up trail again, or you arrive back 
at the camping area. No fee.
Pachaug State Forest/ 
Mount Misery Trail
Rt. 49, Voluntown, CT 06384
860-376-4075, portal.ct.gov/deep
Moderate 3-mile woodland trail; the Blue-
Blazed Pachaug and Nehantic Trails become 
a single path as they ascend Mount Misery 
(411’), with views to the RI border; return via 
same trail. A 9+ mile trail loop can also be 
accessed here. Pachaug State Forest, with 
26,000+ acres, is the largest state forest with 
35+ miles of marked trails. No fee.
WINDHAM/WILLIMANTIC
Allanach-Wolf Woodlands – 
Joshua’s Trust  
165 Back Rd., Windham, CT 06280
860-429-9023, joshuastrust.org
An easy 3.1 mile out-and-back trail on a 
102-acre property managed and owned by 
Joshua’s Trust. Trail is well marked. Property 
features beautiful views of the 22-acre shallow 
pond known as Lake Marie. Fantastic bird 
watching and blinds for photography. No fee.

Beaver Brook State Park
Back Rd. (off Rt. 14), Windham, CT 06280
860-424-3200, portal.ct.gov/deep
Beaver Brook runs through this undeveloped 
401-acre State Park. There are no marked 
trails, and lots of trees. No fees for parking or

Friends of The Garden on the Bridge & 
The Garden Club of Windham  
440 Main St., Willimantic, CT 06226 
gardenclubofwindhamct.org,  
Like us on Facebook! 
The Garden on the Bridge attracts visitors 
from the region and beyond. Built in 1857, 
this stone structure once carried traffic across 
the Willimantic River. Today it offers colorful 
plantings and exquisite views of dams and 
waterfalls. It is convenient to walking, hiking, 
and biking trails, restaurants and other 
amenities. 
Frog Bridge, “Thread City Crossing”
Jct. Main St. (Rts. 32 & 66) and Church St., 
Willimantic, CT 06226
860-465-3000, windhamct.com
Notable for its 11’ tall bronze frogs sitting atop 
giant thread spools, the Frog Bridge connects 
Main St. to Pleasant St. Be careful of traffic. 
No fee.
Garden on the Bridge
Main St. (Rts. 32 & 66), Willimantic, CT 06226
860-465-3000, windhamct.com
Park at Windham Mills Heritage Park.
Built in 1857 as a pedestrian bridge over 
the Willimantic River, this unusual structure 
features granite planters, flowers and trees. 
Great photo ops. No fee.
Victorian Foot Bridge
off Main St. (opposite North St.),  
Willimantic, CT 06226
860-465-3000, windhamct.com
Built in 1906, the only footbridge in the 
eastern U.S. to cross a highway, river and rail 
line, the footbridge connects Main St. and 
Pleasant St. Bring your camera. No fee.
ACCESSIBILITY 
INFORMATION
Not all trails suit the needs of everyone, 
but everyone deserves an opportunity 
to enjoy the outdoors. TLGV’s Trail 
Assessment Team has assessed more than 
40 miles of trails, collecting objective 
information and developing guides to 
help individuals determine whether a trail 
is right for them. In the online Explore 
Guide at TheLastGreenValley.org you can 
search for hiking by town and will find this 
information published within the listings 
of trails that have been assessed. Trails 
that have been assessed and published 
information include:
• Air Line State Park Trail
• Bicentennial Pond Universal Access 
Trail in Mansfield, CT
• Burgess Discovery Trail in  
Sturbridge, MA
• Cat Hollow Park’s Linear Trail
• Hop River Trail
• Heritage Trail in Southbridge, MA
• Killingly River Trail
• Lebanon Town Green Loop in  
Lebanon, CT
• Merrow Meadow in Mansfield, CT
• North Grosvenordale River Walk  
and Park in Thompson, CT
• Putnam River Trail
TLGV is not the only organization working 
to make the outdoors accessible to 
everyone. While the following trails do 
not yet have TLGV’s mobility guides, they 
have been reviewed by others and are 
considered suitable for some people 
with mobility challenges. You will find 
information in this printed Explore Guide 
and online for: 
• Old Growth Trail in Sturbridge, MA
• Preston Community Park Nature Trail  
in Preston, CT

WOODSTOCK
The Beeches Recreation Park
Senexet Village Rd., Woodstock, CT 06281
860-928-6595, woodstockconservation.org
Entrance to trail is near the basketball court 
on the west side of the soccer field. Loop 
trail is about 0.87 miles, flat or gently sloping 
except for 2 sections with slight grade. 
Wraps around a residential development, a 
shady walk with pretty views of Little River 
waterway. Good for cross-county skiing or 
snowshoeing if there is snow. More family-
friendly fun available: basketball court, soccer 
field, playscape, paddling too. See website for 
more. No fee.
Border Woods Preserve –  
The New Roxbury Land Trust  
Eastford Rd. (Rt. 198), north of Rt. 197, 
Woodstock, CT 06281
860-928-2669, nrlt.org
36.7 mostly-wooded acres (lots of oaks & 
hemlocks) open for walking, hiking and bird 
watching. There are no developed trails, some 
erosion and a bit of wetlands, but it’s still a 
great place to explore. Respect the property, 
leaving nothing behind. No motorized 
vehicles allowed. No fee.
The Fifty-Acre Wood
Pond Factory Rd., Woodstock, CT 06281
860-928-0208, woodstockconservation.org
Park by the kiosk at the entrance to the 
trailhead. 50-acre wooded property donated 
to the Town of Woodstock by the Darby family 
in memory of Herbert C. & Virginia Darby in 
2001, features an easy 1.5-mile loop trail plus 
letterboxing. Hunting & motorized vehicles 
are prohibited. Leashed dogs are welcome. 
Please do not litter. No fee.
First Congregational Church of 
Woodstock, UCC & Hill Church 
Labyrinth  
543 Rt. 169, Woodstock, Connecticut 06281 
860-928-7405, firstchurchwoodstock.org
In the backyard of our church, you will find an 
eleven-circuit, left-hand, classical labyrinth. 
It is free and open to the public at all times. 
Labyrinths are used for meditation, for prayer, 
for healing. A labyrinth is a unicursal maze – a 
maze in which you cannot get lost. There is 
one path in. You come back out the same way. 
Walking at a normal pace to the center of this 
labyrinth and out again takes about twelve 
minutes. Visit our website to learn more about 
our open and affirming congregation of the 
United Church of Christ. 
Palmer Arboretum  
523 Rt. 169, Woodstock, CT 06281 
860-928-6595 ext. 310, woodstockct.gov/
arboretum-committee 
A place for education, enjoyment and 
enlightenment. Plants include American 
Sweetgum, Hoops Blue Spruce, Mountain 
Laurel, Large Fothergilla, Azaleas and various 
Cyprus. For complete listing of plants, see 
the downloadable brochure under the 
documents tab of the Arboretum Committee. 
Roseland Park  
205 Roseland Park Rd., Woodstock, CT 06281

roselandpark.org

JUST OUTSIDE THE CORRIDOR
Denison Pequotsepos  
Nature Center  
109 Pequotsepos Rd., Mystic, CT 06355 
info@dpnc.org, dpnc.org 
DPNC is a non-profit environmental education 
center in Mystic, offering year-round

The Last Green Valley is horse country. Horseback riding is allowed on all Connecticut State 
Park and Forest trails, unless posted otherwise, and also allowed on many Massachusetts State 
Park trails and some federal and local properties.

Air Line State Park Trail
Lebanon, Windham, Chaplin, Hampton, 
Pomfret, Putnam, Thompson, CT
860-295-9523, portal.ct.gov/deep
Stretching across eastern Connecticut from 
Thompson to East Hampton, this flat, linear 
gravel & rock trail, formerly a railroad line, is 
ideal for horseback riding. Take in some views, 
enjoy relaxation and solitude. Find parking 
options & locations as well as more info on 
the website. No fee.

Natchaug State Forest/

104 Kingsbury Rd., Eastford, CT 06242

This vast state forest offers not only horseback

Pomfret Horse &

860-617-1863, pomfrethorseandtrail@gmail.

Quinebaug Woods
Dug Hill Rd., Holland, MA 01521
413-245-7108, town.holland.ma.us
Parts of the moderate/short 1.1 mile loop 
trail are suitable for horseback riding, but 
a hike ahead of time will dictate if your 
4-legged friend would like this trail. Follow the 
Quinebaug River. Head up and over a steep 
hillside with rocky outcrops if conditions 
allow. Mixed open canopy along the top of 
the “hogback” ridge, hemlocks dominate 
below, forming dense stands. View remains 
from a cabin built in 1932. Look over Blake 
Hill to the east and Hamilton Reservoir to the 
south. No fee.

Our National Heritage Corridor is known for its abundant pheasant, turkey, waterfowl, small 
game and deer. Hunters must abide by all permit regulations, seasons, designations and safety 
measures for hunting and trapping in CT & MA. Please check each state’s website for the most 
up-to-date information about seasons, specific locations and types of hunting allowed on each 
property. Check the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers’ website before hunting on federal lands. 
Please obey all posted signs.

Explore The Last Green  
Valley Trail Camp
Coventry, Thompson and Beyond
TLGV, Thompson Rec, TEEG and Coventry Rec 
have teamed up to offer a unique trail camp 
in 2022. Campers will have the opportunity to 
hike a variety of trails, their skills developing 
over the course of the week, be able to 
understand trail signs, read a map and use 
a compass when they are done. They’ll 
also be exposed to outdoor STEM-based 
activities, such as learning about water quality 
monitoring, the sun and more. They will learn 
about local history and agriculture and have 
the opportunity to connect to one another 
and the adults assisting the camp in a small 
group environment.

Feel like a treasure hunter and search out the amazing number and variety of geocaches 
and letterboxes hidden along the trails in The Last Green Valley. Learn about letterboxing at 
thelastgreenvalley.org and explore our Letterboxing Trail! More locations are added all the 
time so check back often. We can’t possibly list all the letterbox and geocaching opportunities 
in the Heritage Corridor. Please check the following resources too. Letterboxing.org lists 
active letterboxes throughout Connecticut and Massachusetts. Geocaching.com app for 
smartphones is free and lists many hides in The Last Green Valley. Always bring a trail map 
when searching off-road!

shops, fine dining, beautiful countryside 
and, of course, Old Sturbridge Village. Fact

communities the Sturbridge Townships. And 
if you’re willing to travel a little bit off the 
beaten path, we’re here to reward you with 
some pretty wonderful discoveries.

Lebanon Historical  
Society Museum  
856 Trumbull Hwy. (Rt. 87), Lebanon, CT 06249 
860-642-6579, museum@historyoflebanon.org, 
historyoflebanon.org 
History-themed letterboxes await you in 
Lebanon and its historic green. Explore 
our campus and discover 10,000 years of 
local history and dig into Lebanon’s past 
at your own pace. Visit exhibits, become a 
Revolutionary War soldier in our hands-on 
history room, research a Lebanon ancestor or 
join a volunteer touring our historic buildings. 
Open year-round Wed. - Sat., 12 - 4 pm. Tours 
of the Jonathan Trumbull Jr. House, Beaumont 
House and Pastor’s Library on Saturday 
afternoon’s mid-May - mid-October. Call for 
schedule. Special events include a celebration 
of traditional crafts on the second Saturday 
in June for Connecticut Open House Day and 
our annual antique show on the Lebanon 
Green held the last Saturday in Sept. Visit 
historyoflebanon.org for details on these and 
other programs. 
One of the locations to get your National

Green Valley.

Letterboxing & Geocaching
Mansfield Hollow State Park
Bassetts Bridge Rd., Mansfield, CT 06250
860-928-6121, 860-424-3200, 
portal.ct.gov/deep
At the EarthCache named “After the Ice Age, ” 
observe post-glacial sediments that were 
deposited in the park at the end of the last 
Ice Age. You will observe the shape they give 
to the land and explore how and in what 
sequence the sediments were formed. Look to 
the state and geocaching websites for specific 
activities related to this Earth Cache.
Natchaug State Forest
66 Pilfershire Rd. (Rt. 198), Eastford, CT 06242
860-928-6121, 860-424-3200,  
portal.ct.gov/deep
Letterbox is on the “CCC Trail, ” a blue/yellow 
Connecticut Forest & Park Association trail. 
Approximately 1 mile in, round trip 2 miles, or 
do loop hike of 3.1 miles. Bring a topographic 
map & compass if you do loop hike or get off 
the trail to explore.
Nathan Hale State Forest
off Case Rd., Coventry, CT 06238
860-424-3200, portal.ct.gov/deep
Rather short walk to letterbox, hidden off of 
South River Rd., located on site of an 18th-
century farm & given special attention as an 
archeological site. Don’t remove or disturb 
anything in the area. Dogs must be leashed & 
under control. Don’t disturb wildlife.
Nipmuck State Forest
Rts. 171 & 190, Union, CT 06076
860-684-3430, 860-424-3200
portal.ct.gov/deep
The letterbox lies in a ravine along 
Mashapaug Pond View Trail, west of Bigelow 
Hollow State Park in Union, in the Breakneck 
Block of Nipmuck. Hike in 1 mile, round 
trip is 2 miles, with longer hikes available. If 
unfamiliar with area, drive to trailhead in the 
park and look at the trail system map posted.

Pachaug State Forest
Rt. 49, Voluntown, CT 06384
860-376-4075, portal.ct.gov/deep
Letterbox is off Wyassup Lake Rd, No. 
Stonington, off blue trail near summit of High 
Ledge Lookout. 2 miles round trip — hilly, 
rocky terrain with steep stretches. Plan on 
1.5 hours. May be slippery when wet. Be 
aware of your surroundings. There is also an 
active letterbox, “Gulliver” on the blue trail, 
that starts at the trail entrance just after 
the intersection of Rt. 201 and Rt. 138 in 
Griswold (on the left just after the intersection 
when heading towards RI on Rt. 138). Visit 
letterboxing.org for the clues.

arboretum-committee

Quaddick State Forest
818 Town Farm Rd., Thompson, CT 06277
860-928-6121, 860-424-3200
portal.ct.gov/DEEP/State-Parks/Parks/
Quaddick-State-Park
Letterbox lies off Quaddick Town Farm Rd.

terrain. Plan for 45 minutes. Mosquitoes & 
deer flies can be thick by wet areas. Consider 
bringing bug repellant. Check out the tall & 
straight red pine while you’re there.

You can walk in the footsteps of history throughout the National Heritage Corridor. From 
Native American history to the modern day our region’s museums, historical sites and historical 
societies explore a unique and rich history and culture that separates our National Heritage 
Corridor from all others.

Brooklyn Historical Society Museum & 
Daniel Putnam Tyler Law Office  
25 Canterbury Rd. (Rt. 169), Brooklyn, CT 06234 
860-774-7728, brooklynct.org 
Located behind the majestic equestrian 
statue of Revolutionary War hero General 
Israel Putnam (1718 - 1790) is the Brooklyn 
Historical Society Museum. The museum 
houses the permanent General Putnam 
Gallery whose exhibits “Israel Putnam: 
Facets of a Hero” and “General Israel Putnam 
Remembered” explore the fascinating life of 
the farmer and citizen soldier whose daring 
exploits in a Pomfret wolf den and during the 
French and Indian War made him a legend in 
his own day, and the creation of the statue 
that honors him. The Museum’s Special Exhibit 
    S. Trapp
107

PAGE 108
Museums & Historic Sites
Gallery features changing displays of local 
history. The current exhibit, “Treasures from 
Brooklyn’s Attic, ” showcases some of the 
varied objects of our collections, items such as 
silver spoons and spectacles made by Edwin 
Newbury (1799 - 1872) in his small shop near 
the Brooklyn Green, a Civil War era silk dress, 
ice harvesting equipment, and a circa 1835 
- 1840 oil painting Young Girl with Cat. This 
year there will be an additional display, “The 
History of Brooklyn’s Hillandale Handweavers. ” 
The museum is wheelchair accessible and 
air conditioned. Behind the museum is the 
19th-century law office of Putnam’s great-
grandson Daniel Putnam Tyler (1798 - 1875) 
furnished as it might have been during his 
career, which included his service as a County 
Court Judge and Secretary of State when he 
supervised Connecticut’s first comprehensive 
economic census. Both museums are open 
May 25 through Oct. 9, every Wed. and Sun. 
from 1 to 5 pm, or by special appointment. 
Please call 860-774-7728 to schedule. There is 
no admission charge for either museum.

Coventry Historical Society

The Coventry Historical Society is a non-
profit group dedicated to discovering, 
collecting and preserving whatever pertains 
to the history of the Town of Coventry and 
promoting historical research in all its aspects 
and making available to the members of  
the Society and to the public, the results of 
this research.
Coventry Historical Society – 
Brick School House  
951 Merrow Rd., Coventry, CT 06238 
Built in 1823-25, the school is the center 
of study and research on what school was 
like in the early 20th century up through 
present day schools. Oral histories have been 
collected from people who attended school 
in the one-room schoolhouses in Coventry. 
Many donations of photographs and other 
memorabilia from former students and 
teachers add to the vast collections. The Brick 
School is open to the public on the first and 
third Sunday of the month, May - Oct., from  
1 - 4 pm and on special occasions. Admission 
is free. Donations are gratefully accepted.

Museums & Historic Sites
Coventry Historical Society – Strong 
Porter Museum & Archive  
2382 South St., Coventry, CT 06238  
Strong-Porter Museum circa 1730, is listed on 
the National Register of Historic Places and 
serves as the Historical Society’s Museum. 
Several rooms of the house, as well as several 
outbuildings, including a carpenter shop, 
blacksmith shop, carriage sheds and barn 
are open to the public. Open Sundays, June 
through mid-Oct. Admission is free. 
Denison Pequotsepos  
Nature Center  
109 Pequotsepos Rd., Mystic, CT 06355 
info@dpnc.org, dpnc.org 
DPNC is a non-profit environmental education 
center in Mystic, offering year-round 
programs for all ages. With two locations 
connected by a greenway, the outdoor 
sanctuary encompasses 300 acres and 10 
miles of trails. The main campus features 
natural history exhibits and live birds of prey.
 
 
Denison Pequotsepos Nature

162 Greenmanville Ave. (Rt. 27),

info@dpnc.org, dpnc.org 
DPNC is a non-profit environmental education

programs for all ages. With two locations 
connected by a greenway, the outdoor 
sanctuary encompasses 300 acres and 10 
miles of trails. The Coogan Farm offers a visitor

The Garden on the Bridge attracts visitors 
from the region and beyond. Built in 1857, 
this stone structure once carried traffic across 
the Willimantic River. Today it offers colorful 
plantings and exquisite views of dams and 
waterfalls. It is convenient to walking, hiking, 
and biking trails, restaurants and other 
amenities.

The Governor Samuel Huntington 
Trust/Huntington Homestead  
36 Huntington Rd. (Rt. 14), Scotland, CT 06264 
860-423-1547, info@huntingtonhomestead.
org, huntingtonhomestead.org 
The Huntington Homestead is the 
birthplace of Samuel Huntington, a signer 
of the Declaration of Independence and 
a distinguished statesman during the 
Revolutionary War and early Republic. The 
remarkably well-preserved site includes 
an 18th-century house and is a National 
Historic Landmark on its original foundation 
surrounded by acres of farmland, bordered 
by Merrick Brook, with old-growth trees, 
stone walls, an abandoned road and other 
interesting features. The Governor Samuel 
Huntington Trust, Inc. is the owner of the 
Huntington Homestead Museum, which 
is open to visitors May - Oct. on the 1st & 
3rd Saturday of each month from 11 am 
- 3 pm. Many of these dates have special 
programming listed on the website. The 
worthy goals of the Trust can only be 
met through broad membership in the 
organization. Join! Visit! 
Activities year-round!

Jonathan Trumbull Jr.  
House Museum   
780 Trumbull Hwy. (Rt. 87), Lebanon, CT 06249 
860-642-6579, museum@historyoflebanon.
org, historyoflebanon.org 
Tour the elegant 18th century home of Eunice 
and Jonathan Trumbull Jr. Mr. Trumbull 
served as George Washington’s military 
secretary, Speaker of the House and Governor 
of Connecticut. Visitors can select from a 
menu of self-guided tours and talk with our 
history interpreter about the house and its 
residents. Call for open hours or to make an 
appointment. The museum is owned and 
operated by the Lebanon Historical Society. 
Admission is free.

Joshua’s Tract Conservation &  
Historic Trust  
642 Wormwood Hill Rd.,  
Mansfield Center, CT 06250 
860-429-9023, administration@joshuastrust.
org, joshuastrust.org 
Joshua’s Trust is a non-profit land conservation 
trust protecting 5,000 acres in Northeastern 
Connecticut that will remain open space and 
protected from development forever. Joshua’s 
Trust works in fourteen towns in Windham 
and Tolland counties and cooperates with 
other land trusts beyond this region. We 
maintain 40 miles of walking trails for the 
public to appreciate and enjoy, and two sites 
of historic significance: the Gurleyville Grist Mill 
and Atwood Farm in Mansfield. Explore our 
properties, learn more about what we do and 
find out how to get involved! We hope to see 
you out on the trails soon!

Museums & Historic Sites
Old Sturbridge Village  
1 Old Sturbridge Village Rd.,  
Sturbridge, MA 01566 
800-SEE-1830, 508 347-3362, osvinfo@osv.
org, osv.org 
Old Sturbridge Village, the largest history 
museum in the Northeast, depicts a rural New 
England 1830s town. Visit over 40 original 
buildings including homes, meetinghouses, 
a school, store, farm, water-powered mills 
and trade shops situated on over 200 scenic 
acres. Talk with costumed historians and see 
heritage breed animals.  
One of the locations to get your National  
Park Service Passport stamped in The Last  
Green Valley. 
Optical Heritage Museum  
12 Crane St., Unit #10, Southbridge, MA 01550 
774-230-8016, info@opticalheritagemuseum.
com, opticalheritagemuseum.com 
The Optical Heritage Museum has thousands 
of items within its collections and displays 
including microscopes, lensometers, 
multifocal and antique spectacles. They 
highlight incredible moments in history, and 
without these scientific discoveries our lives 
would be very different. Visit the Optical 
Heritage Museum and take a journey through 
time, discovering the fascinating origins of 
not just the optical industry, but the history 
behind the many simple household items we 
take for granted today.

Samuel Slater Experience  
31 Ray St., Webster, MA 01570 
508-461-2955, admin@
samuelslaterexperience.org, 
samuelslaterexperience.org 
The Samuel Slater Experience brings to life 
the American Industrial Revolution with 
immersive, interactive exhibits featuring 
sight, sound and movement. Travel with 
Samuel Slater as he secretly carries spinning 
technology from England to America, builds 
the first successful water-powered textile mill, 
then establishes the mills that made Webster 
a “boomtown. ”

Friends of Pachaug Forest, Inc.

Pachaug is the largest forest in Connecticut 
with 26,477 acres. This beautiful, bountiful 
forest has offered so much to so many for

far to enjoy boating, backpacking, camping,

Off-Road Vehicles & Snowmobiling
More than 26,000 acres of public land with 
many miles of roads and trails available for the 
shared enjoyment of hikers, snowmobilers, 
motorcyclists and equestrians. The motorcycle 
trails encompass state and town roads, so 
vehicles must be registered and street legal 
and drivers must be licensed. No dirt bikes. 
Some fees..

Natchaug State Forest
66 Pilfershire Rd. (Rt. 198), Eastford, CT 06242
860-928-6121, 860-424-3200
portal.ct.gov/DEEP/State-Parks/Forests/
Natchaug-State-Forest
Turn east off Rt. 198 at sign for Forest 
Headquarters. Proceed across the Natchaug 
River and uphill to a “T” intersection. Parking 
available by turning left at 0.7 miles, lot holds 
25 vehicles. These trails utilize unplowed 
forest roads and seasonal hiking trails with 
a variety of hilly terrain traversing through 
wooded country. Caution is requested when 
crossing town roads or where two-way traffic 
signs are posted. 17 miles of trails. No fee.
Nipmuck State Forest
Rt. 171 & Rt. 190, Union, CT 06076
860-684-3430, 860-424-3200
portal.ct.gov/DEEP/State-Parks/Forests/
Nipmuck-State-Forest-Bigelow-Hollow- 
State-Park
The Nipmuck Forest snowmobile trail 
begins at the first hiker information sign 
on the right after entering Bigelow Hollow 
State Park. Parking for limited number of 
vehicles. Starting in an easterly direction, the 
snowmobile trail traverses an old woods road 
and serves two-way traffic for a short distance. 
The remainder of the run forms a series of 
loops providing considerable adjustment for 
time and distance. 9 miles of trails, moderate 
operator skill required especially in narrow 
passages. Some fees.
Pachaug State Forest
Rt. 49, Voluntown, CT 06384
860-376-4075, 
portal.ct.gov/DEEP/State-Parks/Forests/
Pachaug-State-Forest
More than 26,000 acres of public land with 
many miles of roads and trails available for the 
shared enjoyment of hikers, snowmobilers, 
motorcyclists and equestrians. A series of 
loops permits adjustments for time and 
distance as desired. The trails are an easy 
grade and suitable for novice operators. 
Download the trail map to see all the multi-
use trails and be aware many of the trails are 
also used by horses, mountain bikers and 
hikers. Some fees.

First Congregational Church 
of Woodstock, UCC &  
Hill Church Labyrinth  
543 Rt. 169, Woodstock, Connecticut 06281 
860-928-7405, firstchurchwoodstock.org
In the backyard of our church, you will find an 
eleven-circuit, left-hand, classical labyrinth. 
It is free and open to the public at all times. 
Labyrinths are used for meditation, for prayer, 
for healing. A labyrinth is a unicursal maze – a 
maze in which you cannot get lost. There is 
one path in. You come back out the same way. 
Walking at a normal pace to the center of this 
labyrinth and out again takes about twelve 
minutes. Visit our website to learn more about 
our open and affirming congregation of the 
United Church of Christ.

of beauty and educational as none other” 
(customer quote we hear over and over) and 
PYO Pumpkins. NO pets or alcohol.

Joshua’s Tract Conservation &

642 Wormwood Hill Rd.,

860-429-9023, administration@joshuastrust.

Joshua’s Trust is a non-profit land 
conservation trust protecting 5,000 acres in 
Northeastern Connecticut that will remain 
open space and protected from development 
forever. Joshua’s Trust works in fourteen 
towns in Windham and Tolland counties and 
cooperates with other land trusts beyond 
this region. We maintain 40 miles of walking 
trails for the public to appreciate and enjoy, 
and two sites of historic significance: the 
Gurleyville Grist Mill and Atwood Farm in 
Mansfield. Explore our properties, learn more 
about what we do and find out how to get 
involved! We hope to see you out on the  
trails soon!

The Chamber of Central Mass South &

46 Hall Rd., Sturbridge, MA 01566

Sturbridge is widely known for its great 
shops, fine dining, beautiful countryside 
and, of course, Old Sturbridge Village. Fact 
is, Sturbridge is at the center of eleven 
towns that are filled with unique places 
and experiences. Collectively, we call these 
communities the Sturbridge Townships. And 
if you’re willing to travel a little bit off the 
beaten path, we’re here to reward you with 
some pretty wonderful discoveries. 
The National Heritage Corridor offers some of the best 
paddling to be found, with almost 90 miles of National 
Recreation Water Trails and many more miles on rivers, 
ponds and lakes. From experiences for skilled paddlers 
to family-friendly adventures, The Last Green Valley 
has it. Before taking to the water, learn as much as you 
can about each segment. The lakes and ponds listed in 
this section offer paddling only (no motors allowed). 
More lakes and ponds for paddling are listed in the 
Boating and Fishing section, but be aware that other 
types of boats are also allowed.

on paddling, get a copy of TLGV’s 
Paddle Guide, detailing the National

French River Canoe Trail –  
Greenbriar to Hodges Village Dam
30 Howarth Rd. (off Charlton Rd.)
Oxford, MA 01540, 508-248-5697
thelastgreenvalley.org, 
nae.usace.army.mil/Missions/Recreation/
Hodges-Village-Dam/
Parking available near put-in at Greenbriar 
Park and take-out at Hodges Village Dam. 
This stretch of the French River has only one 
established kayak/canoe launch and the trip 
down to the Bailey Bridge take-out area at 
Hodges Village Dam is not an easy paddle. 
Boaters must portage around at least two 
formidable beaver dams and can count on 
tree snags occurring here and there along 
the way. What makes it worth the effort is 
the abundance of wildlife, particularly in the 
open marsh areas. The trail is 3.4 miles long, 
and should take about 2.5 hours. Ample 
opportunities to stop and pull out for rest 
and exploration. You can also paddle back 
upstream to the put-in. No fee.
Friends of Pachaug Forest, Inc.  
36 Tatro Rd., Griswold, CT 06351 
860-376-5295, friendsofpachaugforest@gmail.
com, friendsofpachaugforest.com 
Pachaug is the largest forest in Connecticut 
with 26,477 acres. This beautiful, bountiful 
forest has offered so much to so many for 
generations. People come from near and 
far to enjoy boating, backpacking, camping, 
cross country skiing, fishing, hiking, horseback 
riding, horse camping, hunting, motorcycling, 
mountain biking, picnicking, scenic vistas, 
snowmobiling and youth group camping. Pets 
can also enjoy the forest if they are leashed.

Rent a stand-up paddleboard or kayak and

Paddling
124
Green leaf indicates member of TLGV

PAGE 125
continued on next page
CAUTION
Paddle at your own risk. Do not use this guide as your only source of navigation 
information. Conditions change constantly, sometimes drastically, on the rivers. It is your 
responsibility to be aware of changing conditions and the abilities of your group, so your 
decisions lead to a safe trip. 
• Paddle Safe, Paddle Smart.
• Wear your life jacket with an attached whistle.
• Carry a cell phone and pack water, snacks, extra clothing and a flashlight.
• Let friends/family know where you will put in and take out and when you expect  
to return.
• Bring an extra paddle, pump and throw lines.
• Check nearby stream gauges for water levels before you go.
• Be sure everyone in your group has the skills necessary to paddle the segment safely.
• When in doubt, don’t go out.
• In case of emergency, dial 911 and try to pinpoint your location in relation to 
launches, visible landmarks and road crossings.
Paddling
Greeneville Dam is 2.5 miles upstream on the 
Shetucket. Head 4 miles downstream on the 
Thames to Trading Cove. Long Island Sound is 
16 miles downstream. No fee.
Old Killingly Pond
194 Pond Rd., East Killingly, CT 06243
killinglyconservation.org, killingly.org
Part of Killingly Pond State Park, this pond has 
canoe/kayak put-in access available for an 
easy paddle. No fee.
Poquetanuck Cove
Drawbridge Rd., Ledyard, CT 06339
thelastgreenvalley.org
Parking for 8 cars at the Quentin Long Car-Top 
Boat Launch. 100 foot carry to the water. 
Poquetanuck Cove is a 2-mile long tidal 
estuary located between the towns of Preston 
and Ledyard. CT DEEP describes the cove as 
the largest and highest quality brackish water 
meadow and cattail marsh on the Thames 
River. The cove is a significant fish and wildlife 
habitat area known for sheltering flocks of 
wintering birds. Bald eagles are a common 
sight during the winter months. No fee.
Poquetanuck Cove
Royal Oaks Dr., Ledyard, CT 06339
thelastgreenvalley.org
Street side parking is available. Usable only at 
high tide. See description of the cove above.
Quinebaug River National Water Trail 
(NRT) – An Overview
Holland, MA to Canterbury, CT
thelastgreenvalley.org
The Quinebaug River National Recreation 
Water Trail offers 45 miles of paddling 
stretching from Holland, MA to Canterbury, 
CT. Many of the segments provide an 
opportunity for family-friendly outdoor 
adventures with a surprisingly remote feel; 
wildlife is abundant and the only traces of 
civilization are the remnants of old mills. 
Before you go, be sure to check the Paddle 
Guide (thelastgreenvalley.org). There are a 
few stretches of challenging whitewater that 
demand serious paddling skills and some 
sections are not safe for anyone to paddle. 
No fee.
Quinebaug River NRT –  
Holland Pond to East Brimfield Lake
Pond-Bridge Rd., Holland, MA 01521
thelastgreenvalley.org
A dozen free parking spaces in the unpaved 
parking lot at the put-in & more in an 
adjacent lot. This segment is within the East 
Brimfield Lake Project of the U.S. Army Corps 
of Engineers and is a designated Watchable 
Wildlife Area. Paddle downstream 3.9 miles 
to East Brimfield Lake Boat Ramp with little 
current, extensive wildlife habitat, 3 picnic 
landings and no portages. Excellent 3-hour 
125

PAGE 126
Paddling
outing for beginning paddlers and families, 
though once you enter the open water of East 
Brimfield Lake, strong winds can be daunting. 
Paddle conditions are best when flow is 
more than 50 cubic feet per second (cfs), as 
measured by the online USGS Streamgage at 
East Brimfield. Below 20 to 30 cfs, it will be a 
scratchy and weedy ride. No fee.
Quinebaug River NRT –  
East Brimfield Lake
Across from 366-398 Sturbridge Rd.
Fiskdale, MA 01581, thelastgreenvalley.org
Plenty of parking at East Brimfield Lake 
Boat Ramp. Because current is not an issue, 
you can paddle upstream to Holland Pond, 
downstream, or both, depending on the 
weather, your time and stamina. No fee.
Quinebaug River NRT –  
West Dudley to Fabyan
11-37 West Dudley Road, Dudley, MA 01571
thelastgreenvalley.org
Launch for West Dudley Pond is adjacent to 
the parking area. There is vehicular access 
and a turn-around to drop off your boat and 
gear. Launch for the West Dudley to Fabyan 
segment is downstream of the rail trail bridge, 
on river right. The impounded river behind 
West Dudley Hydro Dam (also known as 
Paper Mill Dam) provides a pleasant stretch 
of river with current and plenty of water even 
in dry conditions. Paddle around the pond, 
or upstream until either current or scratchy 
rocks convince you to turn back. West Dudley 
Pond is an ideal place for family outings and 
novice paddlers. Keep an eye on the wind as 
the open water is about 0.75 mile long and 
400 feet wide. The paddle below the dam to 
Fabyan includes quickwater, river turns with 
Class I whitewater, small islands with narrow 
channels and fallen trees. You should have 
enough water to paddle with at least 200 cfs 
at the USGS Streamgage at Westville Dam. 
Beware of turbulence from water released 
from the dam to run the powerhouse; it is 
automated and unpredictable. It may get 
boney in the half-mile Rock Garden sections 
between Rt. 131 (Old Turnpike Rd.) and  
Rt. 197 at the state line. You will float 
comfortably again once you pass under Rt. 
197. Be careful as you approach Fabyan Dam 
which is unsafe to shoot. Hug the shore on 
river’s left and enter the marked channel for 
the take-out/portage. No fee.
Quinebaug River NRT –  
Fabyan to West Thompson Lake
622 Fabyan-Woodstock Rd.
N. Grosvenordale, CT 06255
thelastgreenvalley.org,  
nae.usace.army.mil/Missions/Recreation/West-
Thompson-Lake/
Park in small lots on either side of Fabyan-
Woodstock Rd; carry is less than 250 feet. This 
segment is within the West Thompson Lake 
project of the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers 
and is 5.8 miles long with some moving water 
but no rapids, some flat water, extensive 
wildlife habitat and no portages. It is an 
excellent 3 hour outing for paddlers who 
are comfortable with moving water. Before 
you go, check the West Thompson Lake dam 
website for “West Thompson Lake Real Time 
Water Levels;” if you have at least 225 cfs, you 
will have a pleasant ride. Below that level, 
you’ll scrape or have to walk. As you leave the 
shelter of the river and enter West Thompson 
Lake, wind can be a factor. Hug the left shore 
for the final mile to the West Thompson Lake 
Boat Ramp. No fee.
Quinebaug River NRT – 
West Thompson Lake
341-365 Reardon Rd.,  
N. Grosvenordale, CT 06255
thelastgreenvalley.org,  
nae.usace.army.mil/Missions/Recreation/ 
West-Thompson-Lake/
25-30 free parking spaces at the West 
Thompson Lake Boat Ramp; you can drive 
down to the water to unload/load. Paddle 
around the lake and venture upstream to the 
north end where the Quinebaug River enters. 
Winds are changeable and can make paddling 
difficult, especially for inexperienced and 
young paddlers. No fee.

Paddling
Quinebaug River NRT –  
Putnam to Pomfret
80-82 Kennedy Drive, Putnam, CT 06260
thelastgreenvalley.org
About 12 parking spaces. The shoreline is a 
grassy bank with a 1-2 foot drop to the water; 
this can be tricky, depending on water levels. 
The trip to Rt. 101 in Pomfret is 5.9 miles with 
current, some quick-water and flat-water, 
but no portages. It is an excellent 2 hour 
outing for paddlers who are comfortable with 
moving water. This section holds water well 
and may be navigable when other sections 
are too low. Beginners will enjoy this stretch 
with moderate flow of 300 to 600 cfs on the 
USGS Streamgage at Cargill Falls, Putnam.  
No fee.
Quinebaug River NRT –
Riverside Park, Brooklyn
18 Day Street, Brooklyn, CT 06234
thelastgreenvalley.org
River access is at the end of the parking lot 
on the left.The Town of Brooklyn’s Riverside 
Park Canoe Launch is very user-friendly. Both 
novice and experienced paddlers can enjoy 
several hours here. The site is easy to find, 
has good parking, and can be used almost all 
year. It is a good place for fishing and a picnic 
although there are no restroom facilities. 
Paddlers can travel three to four miles 
upstream and then return back downstream 
to the launch site. Caution – do not paddle 
downstream from the launch. No fee.
Quinebaug River NRT –  
Killingly to Plainfield
43 Wauregan Rd., Killingly, CT 06239
thelastgreenvalley.org
Plenty of parking, even if ball games are in 
progress. Park and carry your boat down the 
gravel driveway between the baseball fields 
to the paved Quinebaug River Trail. Turn left 
onto the trail and carry about 100 feet more 
to the kiosk. This is the most challenging 
segment of water trail on the Quinebaug. 
You can paddle 5.3 miles to the CT DEEP Fish 
Hatchery, or continue on to Robert Manship 
Park in Canterbury for a 10-mile outing. Along 
the way, you’ll encounter lots of quick-water, 
several Class II rapids, spiky remnants of 
dams, some flat-water and no portages. It is a 
challenging outing for experienced paddlers. 
With moderate flows, this stretch of the river 
moves swiftly with broad, powerful current. 
No fee.
Quinebaug River NRT –
Plainfield to Canterbury
139-159 Trout Hatchery Rd.,  
Plainfield, CT 06374
thelastgreenvalley.org
When the gate is open, you can drive to the 
launch and drop off or pick up boats, but 
you are not permitted to park there. There is 
plenty of parking outside the gate which is 
several hundred feet from the river. The 4.7 
miles of paddling to Robert Manship Park 
in Canterbury is somewhat less challenging 
    A. Dabrowski
127

PAGE 128
Paddling
than the miles above the hatchery, but 
there are several stretches of white water to 
paddle carefully. Generally, the river is wide, 
undeveloped, tree-lined and very beautiful! 
Watch for the Rt. 14 bridge as you near Robert 
Manship Park. As you approach the take-out 
on river right, stay close to the right bank 
and arrange your group in a line. The broad 
current can make this a tricky landing. Have 
the first paddlers ashore catch subsequent 
boats as they pull in. No fee.
Quinebaug River NRT – Robert 
Manship Park to Butts Bridge, 
Canterbury
39-55 Lovell Lane, Canterbury, CT 06331
thelastgreenvalley.org
At the bottom of the sloping driveway, the 
launch is straight ahead. Plenty of parking 
spaces in that corner of the park. Portable 
restrooms often available. The path to the 
water is moderately sloped and can be 
slippery. After Robert Manship Park, the 
current slows and the river makes five large, 
meandering turns. The lack of current may 
make paddling feel sluggish and you may 
wonder whether the bends will ever end! 5.6 
miles to Butts Bridge. No fee.
Quinebaug River NRT – Butts Bridge  
to Aspinook Pond, Canterbury
67 Butts Bridge Rd., Canterbury, CT 06331
thelastgreenvalley.org
Room for many cars. You can pull up right to 
the water’s edge. The landing is a nearly flat 
firm surface into the flatwater of Aspinook 
Pond. Anglers launch power boats here, too, 
so it can be busy during weekends. No public 
restrooms. The still water of Aspinook Pond 
and easy access make this an excellent place 
for novice paddlers to learn new skills safely. 
The scenery is less dramatic than upriver, but 
you may see everything from bald eagles and 
osprey to wood ducks, Canada geese and 
mergansers. You can paddle downriver on the 
pond almost 3 miles to Jewett City. No fee.
Roseland Park   
205 Roseland Park Rd., Woodstock, CT 06281 
860-963-9557, ssbn598gold@gmail.com, 
roselandpark.org 
Roseland Park sits along the 96-acre Roseland 
Lake open to fishing, boating and paddling. 
Open daily, closes at sunset. No swimming. 
Seven Mile River
Stevens Rd., East Brookfield, MA 01515  
chicopee4riverswatershed.files.wordpress.com/ 
2018/09/quaboag-bt-map-7-31-18-final.pdf
East Brookfield access on the Seven Mile 
River is on Stevens Rd off Cottage St (off Rt 
9). At low flows, the first hundred yards can 
be shallow. The East Brookfield River below 
Lake Lashaway can be accessed at the end of 
Connie Mack Dr. In low water, these segments 
may be best to paddle up from Quaboag 
Pond. Be prepared for beaver dams, too.
Shetucket River National Recreation 
Water Trail (NRT) – An Overview
Windham to Norwich, CT
thelastgreenvalley.org
The Shetucket River National Recreation 
Water Trail provides paddlers with 22 miles 
of paddling enjoyment with four portages. 
Follow the Shetucket River Water Trail starting 
at the Lauter Park Launch in Willimantic and 
ending 22 river miles downstream at Harold 
T. Brown Memorial Park in Norwich. Before 
you go, be sure to check the Paddle Guide 
(thelastgreenvalley.org) for information on 
each segment. No fee.
Shetucket River NRT –  
Lauter Park to Plains Road
619 Jackson St., Willimantic, CT 06226
thelastgreenvalley.org
Plenty of parking along road and for several 
cars near boat launch. Relatively easy put-in 
but beware of large rock at launch location 
depending on water levels. Lauter Park 
is a popular paddling starting point and 
is actually on the Natchaug River which 
quickly meets up with the Willimantic, where 
together they form the Shetucket. Depending 
128
Green leaf indicates member of TLGV

PAGE 129
continued on next page
on water levels the river moves with some 
current but is still relatively easy for novice 
paddlers. The river passes under Rt. 66, makes 
three sharp turns, then flows under the Rt. 14 
Main Street bridge and passes the wastewater 
treatment facility. 3.2 miles to the Plains Road 
landing, just under the Plains Road bridge on 
the western shore (river right.) Be aware of 
current when approaching the landing. No fee.
Shetucket River NRT – Plains Road to 
Salt Rock State Park & Campground, 
Baltic (Sprague)
305-331 Plains Rd., Windham, CT 06226
thelastgreenvalley.org
Ample parking at town rec fields for many 
cars. 50-foot carry to top of riverbank, then 
25-foot long steep incline to the water. This 
7-mile segment is popular & scenic, passing 
railroad tracks, Rt. 32, and finally into a more 
wooded habitat, offering common sightings 
of eagles, osprey, muskrat, otters & more. 
A mile from the launch you’ll approach an 
800-foot island. Paddle to the east side (river 
left) through the wider channel. Pass under 
the Rt. 203 bridge (which can be used for 
an emergency exit only but is not a public 
landing or launch location) and note that 
you will also pass the private Windham Fish 
& Game Club, which is not a public landing 
either. Five miles from the Plains Road Launch, 
paddlers must portage the Scotland Dam. 
DO NOT PADDLE OVER SCOTLAND DAM! The 
portage is on the western shore (river right), 
350 feet long with the first 50 feet up a hill 
beside dam, then down a sloping path to 
launch location below the dam. This is a great 
spot to relax and rest up for the remainder 
of the paddle to Salt Rock State Park. Drier 
summer months can make the stretch below 
the dam very “bony. ” Salt Rock State Park is 
approximately 2 miles from the dam on the 
eastern shore (river left). You can exit the river 
at Salt Rock by carrying your boat 300 feet 
from landing, through tunnel under railroad 
tracks to parking area. No fee.
Shetucket River NRT – Salt Rock  
State Park & Campground to Sprague 
River Park
137 Scotland Rd., Baltic (Sprague), CT 06330
thelastgreenvalley.org
Parking area is 300 feet from river through 
flat pedestrian tunnel running under 
railroad tracks. Paddle the 2.1 miles from 
the campground to the popular small park 
which attracts fly fishermen and is home to 
picnic benches, grills and a portable restroom 
available on site in warmer months. The 
landing on the east side of river (river left) can 
be tricky with exposed rocks on both sides 
of the shore. Depending on water levels, stay 
middle/right of river approaching landing, 
then paddle hard left across current to the 
beach/landing area. No fee.
Shetucket River NRT – Sprague  
River Park to Occum Dam or Red 
McKeon Park
24-78 North Main St. (Rt. 97)
Baltic (Sprague), CT 06330,  
thelastgreenvalley.org
Ample parking in this popular small park. 
Launch from park picnic/beach area unless 
water levels are too low, then launch just 
under the Rt. 97 bridge instead. Although 
wide, launching in this area can be a bit bony 
in low water. Enjoy picturesque coves, inlets 
and lots of wildlife in the river habitat along 
this 2.5-mile section on the way to Occum, 
Paddling
    A. Dabrowski
129

PAGE 130
where paddlers must portage at the Occum 
Dam, which has a 14 foot drop. DO NOT 
PADDLE OVER OCCUM DAM! Approach dam 
and portage on the eastern side (river left). 
Carry approximately 150 feet to the launch 
area below the dam if you’d like to paddle 0.5 
miles further south to Red McKeon Park.  
No fee. 
Shetucket River NRT – Occum Dam or 
Red McKeon Park to Taftville Dam
Occum Dam: 23 Church St.
Baltic (Sprague), CT 06330
Red McKeon Park: 30 Taftville-Occum Rd. (Rt. 97), 
Occum (Norwich), CT 06360
thelastgreenvalley.org
Parking at dam is available adjacent to the 
dam, Mon. – Sat., across from the Versailles 
United Methodist Church (please do not 
park here Sundays from 10 am - noon, as it 
is used for church services.) Launch above 
the dam to paddle up to Sprague River Park 
or launch below the dam to paddle down as 
far as Howard T. Brown Park (7 miles with 2 
portages). Parking also at Red McKeon Park, a 
newer and easier launch just 0.5 further south 
on Rt. 97 with a 300-foot carry down paved 
path to launch. Just over 1.5 mile mostly 
flatwater paddle is easy with just a slight 
current. Pass houses and woods, travel under 
Rt. 395 and past a large trash incinerator 
plant. The views of the Ponemah Mill complex 
and its beautiful towers is something special. 
This is a popular stretch for fishing. Paddlers 
must portage at the Taftville Dam, a large and 
imposing structure 500 feet across the river 
and one of the most difficult on the Shetucket. 
DO NOT PADDLE OVER TAFTVILLE DAM! Take 
out on eastern shore (river left) above the 
dam. This landing is closed to vehicle access 
from Nov. 1 - April 1. No fee.
Shetucket River NRT – Taftville Dam to 
Greeneville Dam
Riverview Dr./Boat Launch Dr. (off Rt. 169)
Lisbon, CT 06351, thelastgreenvalley.org
Parking & launch areas are accessed 
from Riverview Dr. (also the entrance to 
Wheelabrator Technologies Lisbon), and are 
closed to vehicles from Nov. 1 - April 1. Put in 
via several steps with handrails from the top 
of the dam to the rocky put in location below 
the dam. It is a long 700+ feet carry from 
parking, down steps to launch. *This section 
is not recommended for most paddlers due to 
rocky & varying conditions, dramatic changes 
in water levels, plus very bony paddling as 
you approach downtown Norwich. This 2.4-
mile section carries you past the Rt. 12 bridge 
and to the confluence with and the end 
of the 60-mile long Quinebaug River (river 
left). Next, you’ll enjoy pretty habitat on the 
eastern shore (river left) and another popular 
fishing location. Paddlers must portage at 
the Greeneville Dam on the eastern shore 
(river left), which can be very muddy! DO 
NOT PADDLE OVER GREENEVILLE DAM! To 
exit the river, walk through woods to parking 
area along the dirt road off Roosevelt Ave., or 
carry 300 feet to the launch below the dam 
to continue down to Brown Memorial Park. 
No fee.
Shetucket River NRT – Greeneville Dam 
to Howard T. Brown Memorial Park
2-14 Roosevelt Ave., Norwich, CT 06360
thelastgreenvalley.org
Parking for a few cars is located off Roosevelt 
Ave. on a dirt access road adjacent to river 
by the river information kiosk. Access river 
through the woods or carry 1,000 feet to 
the launch area below the dam. This 2-mile 
section takes you through a popular fishing 
area and away from Greeneville Dam’s fish lift, 
which allows migratory fish to move upstream 
from Long Island Sound & the Thames River 
into the Shetucket. Paddle through the City 
of Norwich, past the East Main St. Bridge and 
to Brown Memorial Park where the Yantic & 
Shetucket Rivers combine to form the Thames 
River. Take out in Brown Memorial Park.  
No fee.
Paddling

Shetucket River NRT –  
Howard T. Brown Memorial Park
11-99 Market St., Norwich, CT 06360
thelastgreenvalley.org
ct-norwich.civicplus.com/documentcenter/

Plenty of parking at Brown Memorial Park or

Chelsea Harbor Dr. Paddle the Shetucket, the

point, as you explore the Norwich Harbor 
Water Trail, with maps and information 
available at the website listed above. No fee.

Willimantic River National Recreation 
Water Trail (NRT) – An Overview
Willimantic, CT
thelastgreenvalley.org, willimanticriver.org
The Willimantic River National Recreation 
Water Trail provides more than 22 miles of 
paddling enjoyment and challenges with 
only one short portage. There are 3 major 
segments: the rapids and quickwater of the 
narrow upper section; the impoundment 
above Eagleville Lake Dam; and downstream 
below the dam on slow-moving current or flat 
water to the new landing in Willimantic, CT. 
Before you go, be sure to check the Paddle 
Guide (thelastgreenvalley.org) for information 
on each segment. No fee.
Willimantic River NRT –  
Stafford Springs to Heron Cove
Commuter Lot on Rt. 32
Stafford Springs, CT 06076
thelastgreenvalley.org, willimanticriver.org
15 spaces available on weekends only. This 
challenging segment is best run at medium to 
high water by experienced paddlers. Downed 
trees, old dams, fishermen and rocks are a 
few of the challenges. 6.5 miles to Heron 
Cove. Consult the Paddle Guide for more 
information prior to launching. No fee.
Willimantic River NRT –  
Heron Cove to Merrow Meadow
Heron Cove on South River Rd.,  
Tolland, CT 06084
thelastgreenvalley.org, willimanticriver.org
Parking for many cars. Follow the paved road 
to the left, past the soccer fields to the boat 
launch parking area. From Heron Cove it is 
4.3 miles to the Merrow Meadow (river left) or 
Taylor Preserve Landing (river right). Paddling 
on this stretch of the river is for experienced 
paddlers. You can experience Class II rapids 
or more depending on water conditions, in 
addition to rocks and possibly strainers.
Consult the Paddle Guide for additional 
information prior to launching. No fee.
131

PAGE 132
Paddling
Willimantic River NRT – 
Merrow Meadow to River Park
Merrow Meadow Park on Merrow Rd.,
Mansfield, CT 06268
thelastgreenvalley.org, willimanticriver.org
Parking for 10 or more cars and a good 
location for family outings with walking trails 
and a nicely maintained park. The paddle to 
River Park is 3 miles long and for experienced 
paddlers. You’ll encounter 2 islands on this 
stretch. Approach the second island slowly. 
The quickest route is on the left, but it is 
narrow and can get blocked with trees. The 
right side can be shallow. Below this island, 
after a straight-away, beware of a sharp right 
turn where the current can range from mild 
to strong, pushing you to river left. This area 
has strong eddies and a number of bends. The 
current begins to slow as you pass under Rt. 
44 approaching the River Park landing (river 
left). No fee.
Willimantic River NRT – 
River Park to Eagleville Lake, 
Mansfield
Plains Rd., Mansfield, CT 06268
thelastgreenvalley.org, willimanticriver.org
10+ spaces and a nicely landscaped circular 
drive for easy access unloading boats near 
the launch. River Park in Mansfield is just 
upstream of the point where the river 
becomes an impoundment behind Eagleville 
Dam. Access for car-top carriers is excellent; 
the concrete ramp is smooth and wide and 
the gradual slope to the water’s edge makes 
getting in and out of boats easy. This is 
the best launch site on the river for novice 
paddlers and those unfamiliar with paddling 
in current. The spacious and well-groomed 
launch site, plus the adjacent playing field, 
makes it an ideal site for outings with kids. 
Paddling upstream you will encounter some 
current, but it’s worth the extra effort to 
explore in this direction until the current gets 
the best of you. Since the river becomes an 
impoundment downstream of the launch, this 
is a good place for a loop trip with no worries 
about current; just be aware of wind on the 
lake. 1.4 miles one way to Eagleville Lake. 
To portage Eagleville Dam, take out on river 
left. Parking and a takeout also available river 
right. No fee.
Willimantic River NRT – 
Eagleville Lake to Willimantic
Rt. 275, Mansfield, CT 06268
thelastgreenvalley.org, willimanticriver.org
Parking for a few cars on river right (Coventry) 
or parking for 20 cars river left (Mansfield). 
Use Mansfield lot for put-in below dam; carry 
across Rt. 275 to launch along Eagleville 
Preserve Trail. The river in this segment is 
narrow, wooded, and flows along railroad 
tracks and farms. Watch for low branches, 
downed trees and sand bars, and in high 
flows, partially submerged farm fences. There 
are 6 bridges on this section of river; take 
out river left just after the 6th bridge (Air 
Line Trail foot bridge). You must exit the river 
here to avoid dangerous dams in downtown 
Willimantic. Consult the Paddle Guide for 
more information prior to launching. No fee.
Willimantic River NRT – 
Air Line Trail Landing & Launch
42 Wilson St. (Rt. 66 to Bridge St., immediate
right into lot at sign for CT Eastern Railroad
Museum), Willimantic, CT 06226
thelastgreenvalley.org, willimanticriver.org
Parking for a few cars in small area on left 
side of road approximately 0.5 miles down 
dirt road. Caution: It is not safe to paddle 
downstream (to the left facing the river at 
the launch site), as there is no public landing 
downstream. Constructed and dedicated in 
2016, this launch generally offers an easy 
upstream (to the right facing the river) paddle 
except in high waters or after rain. Paddle 
up 1 mile to the Columbia Rt. 66 bridge, and 
if conditions allow, continue 6 miles further 
upstream to Eagleville. Take inside curves for 
smoother water. With limited development
along this stretch, wildlife viewing 
opportunities exist, such as spotting flying 
eagles & swimming otters. Paddle as far as 
the current or your timetable allows, then 
turn around and return to the Air Line Trail 
Landing. No fee.

Located in a former 1909 post office building, 
Willibrew has been serving up craft brews 
since 1997. Although extensive renovations 
have been made to the building, we’ve 
retained the architecture while creating a 
one-of-a-kind atmosphere for dining.

291 Pond Rd. (Rt. 207), Franklin, CT 06254

countryside. No fee.

Use these descriptions with the chart on the following pages to decide where your next public 
lands adventure will be. 
Connecticut launched a new Passport to Parks program allowing CT residents to enter state 
parks for free. The program is funded by a surcharge on automobile registrations. Visitors 
with out-of-state license plates will still be charged entrance fees at many parks and forests. 
Non-resident season passes are available. See portal.ct.gov/DEEP/State-Parks/Passport-to-the-
Parks for information.
Massachusetts offers season passes for its state parks. MA also offers a one-time fee 
for a lifetime, Senior Parks Pass for MA residents 62 years or older. See mass.gov/visit-
massachusetts-state-parks for information.
State & Federal Parks & Forests
Explore! OUTDOOR ACTIVITIES
Air Line State Park Trail 
Lebanon, Windham, Chaplin, Hampton, 
Pomfret, Putnam, Thompson, CT
860-295-9523, 
portal.ct.gov/DEEP/State-Parks/Parks/ 
Air-Line-State-Park-Trail 
This linear gravel/pebble covered trail is 
flat, easy and travels for almost 50 miles 
in The Last Green Valley. 3 sections: South 
from E. Hampton to Windham (22 miles), 
North from Windham to Pomfret (21 miles), 
and the Thompson section (6.6 miles). The 
4-mile section from Pomfret to Putnam is 
almost complete and has new road crossings. 
Additional refurbishing and regrading is 
ongoing. Putnam is working on connecting 
the trail through town to Thompson. 
Originally used from 1873 through 1955 as a 
Boston to New York railroad, the “Ghost Train” 
was seen traveling this route in earlier days. 
Now a place to frequent in all seasons: hiking, 
biking, cross-country skiing, snowshoeing, 
jogging, horseback riding and even walking 
your leashed dog. Parking locations, section 
maps, videos and historical information 
available on the website. Business-card sized 
z-maps for many of sections of the trail are 
available with each town and at The Last 
Green Valley office. Mobility reference guides 
are also available at TheLastGreenValley.org. 
No fee. 
Beaver Brook State Park
Back Rd. (off Rt. 14), Windham, CT 06280
860-424-3200, portal.ct.gov/deep
Beaver Brook runs through this undeveloped 
401-acre state park. There are no marked 
trails, lots of trees, and hunting is allowed in 
season. No fees for parking or entrance to this 
still very natural park.

Bigelow Hollow State Park
Rt. 171, Union, CT 06076
860-684-3430, 860-424-3200

Hodges Village Dam
30 Howarth Rd. (off Charlton Rd.)

nae.usace.army.mil/Missions/Recreation/
Hodges-Village-Dam/
1,200 varied acres: forest, ponds, wetlands

Hop River State Park Trail
Coventry, Windham, CT
860-424-3200, portal.ct.gov/deep
A former railroad line originating to 1849, 
the trail winds from Vernon over 20 miles 
outside The Last Green Valley before entering 
Coventry and Windham. A new bridge now 
connects this trail in downtown Willimantic 
to the Air Line State Park Trail for miles of 
recreational possibilities across the National 
Heritage Corridor. Parking locations, section 
maps, historical information and more 
available on the website. No fee.

Hopeville-Pond-State-Park

sports (year-round), wooded camp sites, 
picnic tables, trails for hiking & biking. The 
137-acre lake offers swimming, boat launch 
& boating (8 mph limit), fishing (accessible 
platform), paddle over to Pachaug Pond & 
back. Restrooms on site. Main gate open 
seasonally. Some fees.
